1Introduction

The theme of these lectures is the use of stochastic analysis to study a class of random fields defined on a set \(\Lambda =\mathbb{T}^d\) (a \(d\)-dimensional torus) or \(\mathbb{R}^d\) with \(d = 2, 3\). Formally, the fields we are interested in, and which we collectively call Euclidean quantum fields (EQF), are expressed via an integration over all configurations \(\varphi : \Lambda \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) with weight:

\(\displaystyle \exp \left( - \int_{\Lambda} \frac{1}{2} | \nabla \varphi (x) |^2 + \frac{1}{2} | \varphi (x) |^2 + v (\varphi (x)) \mathrm{d} x \right) \mathcal{D} \varphi \) (1)

where \(\mathcal{D} \varphi\) is some kind of infinite-dimensional flat measure (like Lebesgue's, which however does not exist).

This formula, which seems quite arbitrary, has its origin in the fundamental property one would expect these fields to have, i.e. the domain Markov property, which is a natural multidimensional generalisation of the usual Markov property for stochastic processes. Intuitively a Euclidean quantum field theory (EQFT) is a “Markov field” in some \(d\)-dimensional Riemannian manifold. This point of view has been introduced by Nelson [47, 48] and exploited by G. Segal to give an axiomatization of (E)QFT as functors (Pickrell [52] provides a formulation accessible to the probabilist).

The Markovian picture is difficult to make precise in relevant examples, and it has been long understood that an appropriate replacement for Markovianity, which still gives enough structure to the problem, is to assume that EQFTs are reflection positive and Euclidean invariant random fields. For the scope of these lectures I decided to skip the detailed discussion of these properties, suffice it to say that relevant examples we know of random fields satisfying these two properties have the form (1).

A good starting point to study these fields is to start with the case when \(v = 0\). Then the functional integral becomes quadratic and it can be interpreted as a Gaussian measure, which we denote \(\mu\), with covariance given by \((1 - \Delta)^{- 1}\) where \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(\Lambda\). This is the (massive) Gaussian Free Field, an object which has been well studied in the mathematical literature. The Gaussian free field in dimension \(d \geqslant 2\) has only distributional sample paths, belonging locally to the Besov space \(B_{\infty, \infty}^{1 - d / 2 - \kappa} (\Lambda)\) for any \(\kappa > 0\). Moreover given its explicit nature it is quite easy to show that it has indeed some form of the Markov property. See e.g. the very good recent book of Berestycki and Powell [10].

Following the heuristic formula, other EQFTs can be realised as Gibbsian perturbations of the GFF, i.e. exponential tilts of the general form

\(\displaystyle \nu (\mathrm{d} \varphi) = \frac{e^{- V}}{Z} \mu (\mathrm{d} \varphi), \qquad \varphi \in \mathcal{S}' (\Lambda) \) (2)

where the function \(V\) is a local functional of the form

\(\displaystyle V (\varphi) = \int_{\Lambda} v (\varphi (x)) \mathrm{d} x,\)

and \(v : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is called the “potential” of the model.

There are three fundamental problems that a mathematician needs to address to construct a measure \(\nu\) which can “rightfully” express the “intentions” of the heuristic formula (1).

Renormalisation problem

Due to the distributional character of the sample paths of the measure \(\mu\), the definition of \(V\) does not really make sense and an approximation procedure is needed, which in turn will involve a “renormalisation”, i.e. the need to “tune” carefully the function \(v\) as we remove the regularisation. Sometimes this procedure creates a measure which is singular wrt. the GFF, so the formula (2) loses its meaning.

Large field problem

The renormalisation procedure involves several expansions, or other manipulation of the approximate Gibbs measure, which in turn create quantities which are difficult to control probabilistically. Intuitively, the large fluctuations of the field \(\varphi\) should be suppressed both by the Gaussian measure and the “stability” of the interaction potential, but, in concrete proofs, it is usually very difficult to keep track of the stability of the model and one risks losing tightness of the approximate measures.

Infinite volume limit

The natural setting for EQFTs is \(\mathbb{R}^d\), the full Euclidean space with its Euclidean group of transformations (rotations + translations). The massive GFF is well defined on \(\mathbb{R}^d\) (even in \(d = 2\) where the GFF without a mass term is not), but the interaction potential has to be integrated over the full space and this gives rise to a singular measure. Again the formula (2) reveals itself as a poor proxy for the object we try to construct.

Regularisation is usually achieved by introducing a smoothing operator \(R_T : \mathcal{S}' (\Lambda) \rightarrow C^{\infty} (\Lambda)\), which is a symmetric operator which removes fluctuations at spatial scales \(\lesssim 1 / T\) and such that \(R_T \rightarrow \operatorname{Id}\) as \(T \rightarrow \infty\). Usually it can be realised via convolution with a sequence of functions approximating the \(\delta\) function as \(T \rightarrow \infty\), or equivalently, by applying a low-pass filter in Fourier space up to frequencies of the order of \(T\). The scale \(T\) is called usually the “ultraviolet cutoff” (UV cutoff).

Using the smoothing operator, we can construct a regularised measure

\(\displaystyle \nu^T (\mathrm{d} \varphi) = Z_T^{- 1} \exp \left( - \lambda \int_{\Lambda} v_T (R_T \varphi (x)) \mathrm{d} x \right) \mu^{\operatorname{GFF}} (\mathrm{d} \varphi)\)

for a \(T\)-dependent modification \(v_T\) of the interaction potential. This is needed (a posteriori) since usually we must “tune” certain components of the potential in order to have a non-trivial limit as we remove the UV cutoff.

There are various models for which we know this procedure can be achieved when \(d \geqslant 2\):

  1. The \(P (\varphi)_2\) models for \(d = 2\) where \(v_T\) is a polynomial bounded below:

    \(\displaystyle v_T (\varphi) = \varphi^n + r_{n - 1, T} \varphi^{n - 1} + \cdots + r_{1, T} \varphi + r_{0, T}\)

    where we must consider renormalisation constants \(r_{k, T}\) which diverge as \(T \rightarrow \infty\).

  2. The \(\varphi^4_3\) model in \(d = 3\), where \(v_T\) is a quartic polynomial bounded below. Usually taken to be symmetric:

    \(\displaystyle v_T (\varphi) = \varphi^4 - r_T \varphi^2 + c_T,\)

    where both \(r_T, c_T\) have to be taken to diverge as \(T \rightarrow \infty\).

  3. The sine-Gordon model \((\cos (\beta \varphi))_2\) in \(d = 2\), with

    \(\displaystyle v_T (\varphi) = c_T \cos (\beta \varphi)\)

    where \(c_T \geqslant 0\) has to be taken to diverge as \(T \rightarrow \infty\).

  4. The exponential model \((\exp (\beta \varphi))_2\) in \(d = 2\), with

    \(\displaystyle v_T (\varphi) = c_T \exp (\beta \varphi)\)

    where instead \(c_T \geqslant 0\) has to be taken to vanish as \(T \rightarrow \infty\). Combinations of exponentials with different \(\beta\) are also treatable.

The aim of these notes is to illustrate how stochastic analysis can be used to address the problem of existence of the limit \(T \rightarrow \infty\) and of the infinite volume limit \(\Lambda \nearrow \mathbb{R}^d\) for Euclidean fields.

Stochastic analysis of Euclidean fields is a very recent development and there are various ways to interpret it, some of them already being object of courses and lecture notes:

Parabolic SQ

This is the classical approach of Parisi–Wu via a Langevin equation [51]. The target measure is realised as the invariant measure of a stochastic dynamics.

Elliptic SQ

In the elliptic variant, the target measure is realised as a marginal of the solution to an elliptic SPDE. This method has been recently introduced in [1, 2] via the supersymmetric dimensional reduction of Parisi–Sourlas [50].

Canonical SQ

In this case the relevant SPDE is a wave equation, see e.g. [29, 31, 30, 16].

To provide a different perspective we decided to focus here on the variational approach to Euclidean quantum fields initiated in [5] using as basic examples the \((\varphi^4)_{2, 3}\) EQFTs on a bounded domain \(\mathbb{T}^d\), and subsequently to suggest which form it takes in the case of the infinite volume problem on \(\mathbb{R}^2\). We will not consider the case of \(\mathbb{R}^3\) since it has not been worked out in detail. Moreover, we would also like to discuss the case of \((\exp (\beta \varphi))_2\) as in this case we can prove uniqueness of the infinite volume limit and it will allow us also to showcase the construction of other models than those with polynomial interactions.

The variational method has been recently used in various contexts to provide novel results: Bringmann used it to construct singular Gibbs measures [14] and study their invariance under dispersive dynamics [15], Chandra, Gunaratnam and Weber used it to show the existence of phase transition in the \(\varphi^4_3\) model [17], Oh, Okamoto and Tolomeo [49] to prove existence and non-existence of certain Gibbs measures.

The PhD thesis of N. Barashkov [6] describes also how to study the infinite volume limit of the Sine–Gordon model (up to the first threshold) via the variational method including a full verification of the Osterwalder–Schrader axioms. The joint work of S. J. Meyer and the author [32] extends these results to the second threshold using a renormalisation group point of view coupled with the forward-backward stochastic dynamics for the optimal control problem. Moreover in [7] together with Barashkov we provide another approach to describe the most singular part of the \(\varphi^4_3\) measure by approximating the optimal drift of the variational description.

Similar ideas relying on a coupling between the Gaussian free field and an interacting field have been used by Bauerschmidt and Hofstetter to control the maximum of the Sine–Gordon model [8]. More generally, mapping a Brownian motion into a measure of interest has revealed itself a useful method also in the context of functional inequalities, see e.g. the work of Borell [11], Lehec [42] and the recent paper of Mikulincer and Shenfeld [43] on the “Brownian transport map”.

Let us finally mention the manifesto [4] where a (speculative) proposal for a new class of stochastic equations, Wilson–Ito diffusions, describing local random fields, including EQFTs is laid out.

The plan of the lectures is the following:

  1. We introduce the Gaussian Free field as a generalised Gaussian Markov process and discuss its regularity and a simple construction of \(\varphi^4_2\) in finite volume, due to Nelson.

  2. We introduce the variational characterisation of an EQFT in terms of a stochastic control problem, via the formula of Boué–Dupuis. This will allow us to transform a problem in probability theory into a problem of stochastic analysis amenable to functional analytic techniques.

  3. We will illustrate these analytic techniques via the construction of \(\varphi^4_2\) in finite volume, providing an alternative proof to that of Nelson.

  4. We shift to the infinite volume limit problem via variational techniques, where new ideas are needed which shift the attention to the Euler–Lagrange equations satisfied by the minimisers of the variational problem. We will discuss how to obtain a-priori information on the EQFTs via these equations in the case of the \(\varphi^4_2\) and exponential models.

  5. We will finish by focusing on the more challenging situation of the \(\varphi^4_3\) model in finite volume where the variational problem has to be “renormalised” to provide a description which is stable in the UV limit.

2Gaussian Free Field

To discuss the Gaussian free field, I find it interesting to start with its one dimensional version, which is the Gaussian Markov process known as the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process, i.e. the stationary solution to the SDE:

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} X_t = - X_t \mathrm{d} t + \mathrm{d} B_t, \qquad t \in \mathbb{R},\)

which can be expressed as the Wiener integral

\(\displaystyle X_t = \int_{- \infty}^t e^{- (t - s)} \mathrm{d} B_s, \qquad t \in \mathbb{R},\)

or as the Gaussian process with covariance

\(\displaystyle C (t - s) =\mathbb{E} [X_t X_s] = \frac{1}{2} e^{- | t - s |}, \qquad t, s \in \mathbb{R}.\)

This process is Markov and its covariance has the Fourier transform given by

\(\displaystyle C (t) = \int_{\mathbb{R}} \frac{e^{i \omega t}}{1 + \omega^2} \frac{\mathrm{d} \omega}{2 \pi}\)

as can be checked via an application of the residue formula. This means that the covariance \(C\) is a fundamental solution for the operator \((1 - \partial_t^2)\).

By analogy, the multidimensional version \((\varphi (x))_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d}\) of this random field can be defined as the Gaussian field on \(\mathbb{R}^d\) whose covariance \(C (x)\) has the Fourier transform given by

\(\displaystyle C (x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \frac{e^{i k \cdot x}}{1 + | k |^2} \frac{\mathrm{d} k}{(2 \pi)^d}\)

which gives a fundamental solution of the operator \((1 - \Delta)\) where \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(\mathbb{R}^d\). However, when \(d \geqslant 2\) we have that \(C (0) = + \infty\) and more generally that

\(\displaystyle C (x) \approx \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} - \log | x | & (d = 2)\\ | x |^{2 - d} & (d > 2) \end{array} \right., \qquad | x | \ll 1,\)

which means that the random field \(\varphi\) does not have continuous sample paths and it must be realised as a generalised function taking values a.s. in \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\), the space of tempered distributions.

The rigorous definition of GFF is therefore that of a Gaussian process indexed by \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d)\) and such that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\varphi (f)^2] = \langle f, (1 - \Delta)^{- 1} f \rangle, \qquad f \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d)\)

where we denoted by \((1 - \Delta)^{- 1}\) the operator whose \(L^2\)-kernel is given by \(C\). We denote by \(\mu = \mu^{\operatorname{GFF}}\) the law of the GFF as a probability measure on \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\). Later on we will consider also the GFF on \(\mathbb{T}^d = [- 1 / 2, 1 / 2]^d\) (with periodic b.c.) which has a similar definition.

If we denote by \(C_{\Lambda}\) the covariance of the GFF on \(\Lambda =\mathbb{T}^d\) or \(\mathbb{R}^d\) then they are related by the method of images:

\(\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{T}^d} (x) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}^d} C_{\mathbb{R}^d} (x + k)\)

where the sum converges as \(C_{\mathbb{R}^d}\) has exponential decay for large distances.

2.1Besov spaces

Besov spaces provide a convenient setting to measure regularity of random fields. For simplicity we discuss the case of \(\mathbb{R}^d\) and discuss later how to adapt it to the case of \(\mathbb{T}^d\). The reader can find full details and proofs in [3, 28] and for weighted spaces in [26, 45].

Let \(\langle \alpha \rangle := (1 + | \alpha |^2)^{1 / 2}\). Let \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d)\) be the Schwartz space of test functions on \(\mathbb{R}^d\) and \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\) the corresponding space of tempered distributions. For any \(\alpha \in \mathbb{R}\) we define fractional derivatives \(\langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{\alpha} = (1 + \mathrm{D}^2)^{\alpha / 2}\) on \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\) by

\(\displaystyle \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{\alpha} f = \mathcal{F} \mathcal{}^{- 1} (\xi \mapsto \langle \xi \rangle^{\alpha} (\mathcal{F} f) (\xi)), \qquad f \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\)

where \(\mathcal{F}\) is the Fourier transform on \(\mathbb{R}^d\). More generally if \(h : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is a nice radial function we let

\(\displaystyle h (\mathrm{D}) f = \mathcal{F} \mathcal{}^{- 1} (\xi \mapsto h (\xi) (\mathcal{F} f) (\xi)), \qquad f \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) .\)

To localise functions in frequency space, we need a partition of unity whose existence can be proved but we shall take this for granted here. Let \(\chi, \varrho\) be smooth radial functions \(\mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) such that

  • \(\operatorname{supp} \chi \subseteq B (0, 4 / 3)\), \(\operatorname{supp} \varrho \subseteq \{ 3 / 4 \leqslant | \xi | \leqslant 8 / 3 \}\);

  • \(0 \leqslant \chi, \varrho \leqslant 1\), \(\chi (\xi) + \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \varrho (2^{- j} \xi) = 1\) for any \(\xi \in \mathbb{R}^d\);

  • \(\operatorname{supp} \varrho (2^{- j} \cdot) \cap \operatorname{supp} \varrho (2^{- i} \cdot) = \varnothing\) if \(| i - j | > 1\).

Introduce the notations \(\varrho_{- 1} = \chi\), \(\varrho_j = \varrho (2^{- j} \cdot)\) for \(j \geqslant 0\).

Given such a partition of unity, we define the associated Littlewood–Paley–projectors as

\(\displaystyle \Delta_j = \varrho_j (\mathrm{D}), \qquad j \geqslant - 1,\)

and write

\(\displaystyle K_j = \mathcal{F}^{- 1} \varrho_j, \qquad j \geqslant - 1,\)

for the resulting convolution kernels in position space.

We can then write, for any \(u \in S' (\mathbb{R}^d)\),

\(\displaystyle u = \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} \Delta_j u = \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} K_j \ast u. \) (3)

where convergence is in the sense of \(S' (\mathbb{R}^d)\).

Note that \(\Delta_j u\) is now a holomorphic function since its Fourier transform is supported on a ball (for \(j = - 1\)) or on an annulus (for \(j \geqslant 0\)), indeed we have for any \(u \in S' (\mathbb{R}^d)\)

\(\displaystyle (\Delta_j u) (x) := \mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varrho_j \hat{u} (\cdot)) .\)

From (3) we finally define the Besov spaces.

Definition 1. Let \(s \in \mathbb{R}, p, q \in [1, \infty]\). For a Schwartz distribution \(f \in \mathscr{S}' (\Lambda)\) define the norm

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B_{p, q}^s} = \| (2^{j s} \| \Delta_j f \|_{L^p})_{j \geqslant - 1} \|_{\ell^q}\)

where \(\| \|_{L^p}\) denotes the normalised \(L^p (\Lambda)\) norm. The space \(B^s_{p, q}\) is the set of functions \(f \in \mathscr{S}' (\Lambda)\) such that \(\| f \|_{B_{p, q}^s} < \infty\) moreover \(H^s = B^s_{2, 2}\) are the usual Sobolev spaces, and we denote by \(\mathscr{C}^s\) the closure of smooth functions in the \(B_{\infty, \infty}^s\) norm.

Remark 2.

  • The specific norm \(\| \cdot \|_{B^s_{p, q}}\) depends on the partition of unity, but all such norms are equivalent and the space \(B^s_{p, q}\) does not depend on the specific choice.

  • The space \((B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{R}^d), \| \cdot \|_{B^s_{p, q}})\) is a Banach space.

Let us quickly note that the parameter \(s \in \mathbb{R}\) indeed measures regularity in the usual sense.

Theorem 3. For any \(s, \alpha \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(u \in S' (\mathbb{R}^d)\)

\(\displaystyle \| u \|_{B^s_{p, q}} \approx \| (1 - \Delta)^{\alpha / 2} u \|_{B^{s - \alpha}_{p, q}} .\)

Remark 4. There is a relation between Besov spaces and Sobolev and Holder spaces: in particular one can prove that,

\(\displaystyle B_{p, p}^s (\mathbb{R}^d) = W^{s, p} (\mathbb{R}^d), \qquad s \in \mathbb{R}_+ \setminus \mathbb{Z} \qquad B_{\infty, \infty}^{\alpha} (\mathbb{R}^d) = C^{\alpha} (\mathbb{R}^d), \qquad \alpha \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Z}.\)

Easy embeddings among Besov spaces are as follows:

  • Since \(\ell^{q_1} (\mathbb{Z}) \hookrightarrow \ell^{q_2} (\mathbb{Z})\) for \(q_1 \leqslant q_2\), we directly see \(B^s_{p, q_1} (\mathbb{R}^d) \hookrightarrow B^s_{p, q_2} (\mathbb{R}^d)\).

  • Moreover, for \(s_2 \leqslant s_1\), \(2^{q s_2 j} \leqslant 2^{qs_1 j}\) so that \(B^{s_1}_{p, q} (\mathbb{R}^d) \hookrightarrow B^{s_2}_{p, q} (\mathbb{R}^d)\).

More generally, functions with localised Fourier transform have equivalent Lebesgue norms (this is called Bernstein's theorem) and this generates embeddings similar to the well-known Sobolev embeddings:

Lemma 5. Let \(1 \leqslant p_1 \leqslant p_2 \leqslant \infty\), \(1 \leqslant q_1 \leqslant q_2 \leqslant \infty\). If \(s_2 \leqslant s_1 - d \left( \frac{1}{p_1} - \frac{1}{p_2} \right)\), then

\(\displaystyle B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1} (\mathbb{R}^d) \hookrightarrow B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2} (\mathbb{R}^d) .\)

Besov spaces on \(\mathbb{T}^d\) have a similar definition using the LP decomposition on the torus.

All along the paper we need to consider Banach subspaces of \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\) associated with a positive spatial weight \(\rho\).

Definition 6. A polynomial weight \(\rho\) is a function \(\rho : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb{R}_+\) of the form \(\rho (x) = c \langle \ell x \rangle^{- \sigma}\) for \(\sigma, c, \ell \geqslant 0\). For a polynomial weight \(\rho\) let

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{L^p (\rho)} = \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} | f (x) \rho (x) |^p \mathrm{d} x \right)^{1 / p}\)

and denote by \(L^p (\rho)\) the space of functions for which this norm is finite. For functions defined on a torus in \(\mathbb{R}^d\) we consider their periodic extensions on \(\mathbb{R}^d\). Similarly we define the weighted Besov spaces \(B_{p, q}^s (\rho)\) as the set of elements of \(\mathscr{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\) for which the norm

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B_{p, q}^s (\rho)} = \| (2^{j s} \| \Delta_j f \|_{L^p (\rho)})_{j \geqslant - 1} \|_{\ell^q}\)

is finite and by \(\mathscr{C}^s (\rho)\) those such that the norm

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{\mathscr{C}^s (\rho)} = \| (2^{j s} \| \rho \Delta_j f \|_{L^{\infty}})_{j \geqslant - 1} \|_{\ell^{\infty}}\)

is finite.

We will fix a weight \(\rho\) such that for some sufficiently small \(\varepsilon > 0\) we have

\(\displaystyle | \Delta \rho^{1 / 2} | + | \nabla \rho^{1 / 2} | \leqslant \varepsilon \rho^{1 / 2}, \qquad \rho \lesssim \langle \cdot \rangle^{- 32} . \) (4)

The number \(\varepsilon\) can depend on various parameters but not on the volume cutoffs we introduce to regularise the Euclidean QFTs.

We note that the weighted norm \(B_{p, q}^s (\rho^{\alpha})\) is equivalent to

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B_{p, q}^s (\rho^{\alpha})}^q = \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{i s q} \| \Delta_i f \|_{L^p (\rho^{\alpha})}^q = \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{i s q} \| \rho^{\alpha} \Delta_i f \|_{L^p (\mathbb{R}^d)}^q \approx \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{i s q} \| \Delta_i (\rho^{\alpha} f) \|_{L^p (\mathbb{R}^d)}^q,\)

that is, the weight \(\rho^{\alpha}\) can be freely commuted with the LP projectors as far as Besov norms are concerned.

We collect here some basic estimates which will be useful below.

Proposition 7. Let \(\delta > 0\). We have for any \(q_1, q_2 \in [1, \infty], q_1 < q_2\)

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B_{p, q_2}^s} \leq \| f \|_{B_{p, q_1}^s} \lesssim \| f \|_{B_{p, \infty}^{s + \delta}} .\)

Furthermore, if we denote by \(W^{s, p}\) the normalised fractional Sobolev spaces then for any \(q \in [1, \infty]\)

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B_{p, q}^s} \lesssim \| f \|_{W^{s + \delta, p}} \lesssim \| f \|_{B_{p, \infty}^{s + 2 \delta}} .\)

Proposition 8. For any \(s_1, s_2 \in \mathbb{R}\) such that \(s_2 < s_1\), any \(p, q \in [1, \infty]\) the Besov space \(B^{s_1}_{p, q}\) is compactly embedded into \(B^{s_2}_{p, q}\).

Definition 9. A smooth function \(\eta\) is said to be an \(S^m\) multiplier if for every multi-index \(\alpha\) there exists a constant \(C_{\alpha}\) such that

\(\displaystyle \quad \left| \frac{\partial^{\alpha}}{\partial \xi^{\alpha}} \eta (\xi) \right| \lesssim_{\alpha} (1 + | \xi |)^{m - | \alpha |}, \qquad \xi \in \mathbb{R}^d .\) (5)

We say that a family \(\eta_t\) is a uniformly \(S^m\) multiplier if (5) is satisfied for every \(t\) with \(C_{\alpha}\) independent of \(t\).

Proposition 10. Let \(\eta\) be an \(S^m\) multiplier, \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p, q \in [1, \infty]\), and \(f \in B_{p, q}^s\), then

\(\displaystyle \| \eta (\mathrm{D}) f \|_{B_{p, q}^{s - m}} \lesssim \| f \|_{B_{p, q}^s} .\)

Furthermore the constant depends only on \(s, p, q, d\) and the constants \(C_{\alpha}\) in eq. (5).

For a proof see [3] Lemma 2.78.

Proposition 11. Let \(\theta \in [0, 1]\), \(p, p_1, p_2\) and \(s, s_1, s_2\) be such that \(\frac{1}{p} = \frac{\theta}{p_1} + \frac{1 - \theta}{p_2}\) and \(s = \theta s_1 + (1 - \theta) s_2\) and assume that \(f \in W^{s_1, p_1} \cap W^{s_2, p_2}\). Then

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{W^{s, p}} \leqslant \| f \|^{\theta}_{W^{s_1, p_1}} \| f \|^{1 - \theta}_{W^{s_2, p_2}} .\)

For a proof see [44].

2.2Regularity of the GFF

The basic argument to obtain a Besov regularity for the GFF is to compute the following average:

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\| \varphi \|_{B^{\alpha}_{2 p, 2 p} (\mathbb{T}^d)}^{2 p}] .\)

We have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\| \varphi \|_{B^{\alpha}_{2 p, 2 p}}^{2 p}] =\mathbb{E} \left[ \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 p \alpha j} \| \Delta_j \varphi \|^{2 p}_{L^{2 p}} \right] = \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 p \alpha j} \mathbb{E} [\| \Delta_j \varphi \|^{2 p}_{L^{2 p}}] .\)

Now we observe that, by Fubini theorem and hypercontractivity of Gaussian vectors (see e.g. Janson's book on Gaussian measures [38])

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\| \Delta_j \varphi \|^{2 p}_{L^{2 p}}] \lesssim (\mathbb{E} [\| \Delta_j \varphi \|^2_{L^2}])^p,\)

so we reduced ourselves to computing covariances. Now we observe that, by standard Fourier analysis

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\| \Delta_j \varphi \|^2_{L^2}] = \sum_{k, k' \in 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}^d} \varrho_j (k) \varrho_j (k') \mathbb{E} [\hat{\varphi} (k) \overline{\hat{\varphi} (k')}] = \sum_{k \in 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}^d} \frac{\varrho_j^2 (k)}{1 + | k |^2}\)

But now the sum is over \(2^{jd}\) points thanks to the support of \(\varrho_j (k)\) and in addition this forces \(| k |\) to be of order \(2^j\), these two remarks give

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\| \Delta_j \varphi \|^2_{L^2}] \lesssim 2^{jd} 2^{- 2 j} = 2^{j (d - 2)} .\)

This means that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\| \varphi \|_{B^{\alpha}_{2 p, 2 p}}^{2 p}] \lesssim \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 p \alpha j} \mathbb{E} [\| \Delta_j \varphi \|^{2 p}_{L^{2 p}}] \lesssim \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 p \alpha j} 2^{jp (d - 2)} = \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 pj (\alpha + d / 2 - 1)} .\)

So we see that in order to have a convergent series we need

\(\displaystyle \alpha + d / 2 - 1 < 0 \qquad \Rightarrow \qquad \alpha < \frac{2 - d}{2} .\)

To conclude, we shall use Besov embedding recalling that if \(1 \leqslant p_1 \leqslant p_2 \leqslant \infty\), \(1 \leqslant q_1 \leqslant q_2 \leqslant \infty\), \(s_2 \leqslant s_1 - d \left( \frac{1}{p_1} - \frac{1}{p_2} \right)\), then

\(\displaystyle B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1} (\mathbb{T}^d) \hookrightarrow B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2} (\mathbb{T}^d) .\)

We are interested in the case

\(\displaystyle p_2 = q_2 = \infty, \qquad p_1 = q_1 = 2 p, \qquad s_1 = \alpha\)

then we see that

\(\displaystyle B^{\alpha}_{2 p, 2 p} (\mathbb{T}^d) \hookrightarrow B^s_{\infty, \infty} (\mathbb{T}^d) = C^s (\mathbb{T}^d),\)

for any \(s \leqslant \alpha - \frac{d}{2 p}\). But since \(p\) can be chosen to be arbitrarily large, we conclude that the above embedding is true for any

\(\displaystyle s < \alpha < - \frac{d - 2}{2} .\)

Remark 12. We see that:

  • for \(d = 1\) the GFF can be realised as a continuous function, indeed it belongs to \(C^{\alpha}\) for any \(\alpha < 1 / 2\);

  • for \(d = 2\), the GFF can be realised as a random distribution which is not very singular, namely \(C^{\alpha}\) for any \(\alpha < 0\), so arbitrarily close to \(0\). From the viewpoint we discussed here, this is why the \(\varphi^4\) model is less difficult in 2 dimensions wrt the higher dimensional case;

  • for \(d \geqslant 3\) we have a singular behaviour getting progressively worse with \(d\).

2.3Regularity of the Wick powers

Since the GFF is a random distribution, it is not easy to construct local non-linear functionals. The non-trivial fact is that they indeed exist and can be constructed by approximations.

Consider a mollifier operator \(R_N\) (see below for precise definitions), e.g. one can take

\(\displaystyle R_N = \Delta_{\leqslant N} = \sum_{i \leqslant N} \Delta_i\)

via partial sums of LP blocks. Then, if \(\varphi\) is a GFF,

\(\displaystyle \varphi_N := R_N \varphi\)

is a smooth Gaussian process with a translation invariant covariance given by \(C_N = R_N (1 - \Delta)^{- 1} R_N^{\ast}\). A direct computation, e.g. in Fourier space, shows that

\(\displaystyle C_N (x) \propto \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} - \log (| x | + N^{- 1}) & \qquad d = 2\\ (| x | + N^{- 1})^{2 - d} & \qquad d \geqslant 3 \end{array} \right.\)

Recall also that the Hermite polynomials \((H_n (x))_{n \geqslant 0}\) are the orthogonal polynomials of the standard Gaussian measure over \(\mathbb{R}\) and that the first of them (in probabilist's normalisation) are:

\begin{eqnarray*} H_0 (x) & = & 1,\\ H_1 (x) & = & x,\\ H_2 (x) & = & x^2 - 1,\\ H_3 (x) & = & x^3 - 3 x,\\ H_4 (x) & = & x^4 - 6 x^2 + 3,\\ H_5 (x) & = & x^5 - 10 x^3 + 15 x,\\ & \vdots & \end{eqnarray*}

generated by the series

\(\displaystyle \exp \left( t x - \frac{t^2}{2} \right) = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} \frac{t^n}{n!} H_n (x) .\)

We define now the (regularised) Wick powers of the GFF as

\(\displaystyle \llbracket (\varphi_N)^n (x) \rrbracket := C_N^{n / 2} (0) H_n (\varphi_N (x) / C_N^{1 / 2} (0)) .\)

For example:

\(\displaystyle \llbracket (\varphi_N)^2 (x) \rrbracket = \varphi_N^2 (x) - C_N (0)\)
\(\displaystyle \llbracket (\varphi_N)^3 (x) \rrbracket = \varphi_N^3 (x) - 3 \varphi_N (x) C_N (0)\)
\(\displaystyle \llbracket (\varphi_N)^4 (x) \rrbracket = \varphi_N^4 (x) - 6 \varphi_N^2 (x) C_N (0) + 3 C_N^2 (0) .\)

In particular the covariance of these local functions of \(\varphi_N\) is given by

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\llbracket (\varphi_N)^n (x) \rrbracket \llbracket (\varphi_N)^k (y) \rrbracket] = \delta_{n, k} (n!) C_N^n (x - y)\)

and by hypercontractivity we also have, for any nice test function \(f : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\)

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \left[ \left| \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \llbracket (\varphi_N)^n (x) \rrbracket f (x) \mathrm{d} x \right|^p \right] \lesssim_{p, n} \left\{ \mathbb{E} \left[ \left| \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \llbracket (\varphi_N)^n (x) \rrbracket f (x) \mathrm{d} x \right|^2 \right] \right\}^{p / 2}\)
\(\displaystyle \lesssim \left| \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} C_N^n (x - y) f (x) f (y) \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} y \right|^{p / 2} .\)

We will need to estimate the average size of the random fields \(\llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket\) uniformly in \(N\). As with the GFF, we do not expect them to be bounded in some function space and therefore we will use negative regularity Besov–Hölder spaces to estimate them. First we note that by Besov embedding, for all \(\alpha \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(N \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \| \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket \|_{C^{\alpha} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p \lesssim \| \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket \|_{B^{\alpha + d / p}_{p, p} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p = \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{i \left( \alpha + \frac{d}{p} \right) p} \int \rho^{\varepsilon p} (x) | \Delta_i \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (x) |^p \mathrm{d} x\)

Taking expectations and using Fubini's theorem we have now

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket \|_{C^{\alpha} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p \lesssim \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{i \left( \alpha + \frac{d}{p} \right) p} \int \rho^{\varepsilon p} (x) \mathbb{E} [| \Delta_i \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (x) |^p] \mathrm{d} x\)

Now Gaussian hypercontractivity applied to the Gaussian polynomial \(\Delta_i \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (x)\), gives

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [| \Delta_i \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (x) |^p] \lesssim \{ \mathbb{E} [| \Delta_i \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (x) |^2] \}^{p / 2}\)

which reduces the computation to an \(L^2\) bound. Now, when \(d = 2\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [| \Delta_i \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (x) |^2] = \int \int K_i (x - y) K_i (x - y') \mathbb{E} [\llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (y) \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket (y')] \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} y'\)
\(\displaystyle \propto \int \int K_i (x - y) K_i (x - y') \{ \mathbb{E} [\varphi_N (y) \varphi_N (y')] \}^n \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} y'\)
\(\displaystyle \lesssim \int \int K_i (x - y) K_i (x - y') | \log^n | y - y' | | \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} y'\)
\(\displaystyle \lesssim \int \int K_i (y) K_i (y') | \log^n | y - y' | | \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} y'\)
\(\displaystyle \lesssim \left[ \int \int (K_i (y) K_i (y'))^{q / (q - 1)} \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} y' \right]^{(q - 1) / q} \lesssim 2^{4 i / q}\)

where we used that in two dimensions

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\varphi_N (y) \varphi_N (y')] \lesssim | \log [| y - y' | + N^{- 1}] | \lesssim | \log [| y - y' |] |\)

and that this function is in \(L^q\) for the product measure \(\mathrm{d} y \times \mathrm{d} y'\) for any \(q \geqslant 1\).

Putting all together we arrive at

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket \|_{C^{\alpha} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p \lesssim \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{i \left( \alpha + \frac{2}{p} + \frac{2}{q} \right) p} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \rho^{\varepsilon p} (x) \mathrm{d} x < + \infty\)

for any \(\alpha < 0\) as soon as \(p\) and \(q\) are large enough, since then we have also \(\rho^{\varepsilon p} \in L^1 (\mathbb{R}^2)\).

This sketches the proof that in \(d = 2\), for all \(\kappa > 0, \varepsilon > 0\) we have, for any \(n \geqslant 1\) and any \(p \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_N \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \kappa} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p < + \infty . \) (6)

A variant of these computations shows that

\(\displaystyle \sup_{M \geqslant N} \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket - \llbracket \varphi_M^n \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \kappa - 1} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p \lesssim N^{- p},\)

that is, the family \((\llbracket \varphi_N^n \rrbracket)_N\) is Cauchy and converges in \(L^p\) to a (unique) limit denoted \(\llbracket \varphi^n \rrbracket \in C^{- \kappa} (\rho^{\varepsilon})\) and called the \(n\)-th Wick power of the GFF.

Similar computations in \(d = 3\) show that for all \(\kappa, \varepsilon > 0\) and \(p \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_N \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket \varphi_N^2 \rrbracket \|_{C^{- 1 - \kappa} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p < + \infty,\)

and similarly can be used to establish the existence of the random field \(\llbracket \varphi^2 \rrbracket \in C^{- 1 - \kappa}\). However for any \(\alpha > 3 / 2, \varepsilon > 0\) we have for \(n = 3\):

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket \varphi_N^3 \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \alpha} (\rho^{\varepsilon})}^p \approx (\log N)^{p / 2}\)

which hints at the fact that in \(d = 3\) the family \((\llbracket \varphi_N^3 \rrbracket)_N\) does not converge to a well-defined random distribution. A similar phenomenon affects all the higher Wick powers. In \(d = 4\) things get worse and not even \(\llbracket \varphi^2 \rrbracket\) exists.

2.4Nelson's approach to \(\varphi^4_2\)

To illustrate the basic ideas of the construction of Euclidean QFTs let us review the proof of Nelson that the \(\varphi^4_2\) model exists in finite volume. Consider the functional

\(\displaystyle V_T (\varphi) = \int_{\Lambda} [\varphi^4 (x) - r_T \varphi^2 (x)] \mathrm{d} x\)

and let's try to take the limit \(T \rightarrow \infty\) for the family of measures

\(\displaystyle \nu^T (\mathrm{d} \varphi) = \frac{\exp (- \lambda V_T (R_T \varphi))}{Z_T} \mu (\mathrm{d} \varphi),\)

for some \(\lambda > 0\). We let

\(\displaystyle r_T = 6\mathbb{E} [(R_T \varphi (x))^2]\)

where we denote by \(\mathbb{E}\) the expectation wrt the measure \(\mu = \mu^{\operatorname{GFF}}\) with canonical process \(\varphi\).

In this case, writing \(c_T := \mathbb{E} [(R_T \varphi (x))^2] = r_T / 6\) (independent of \(x\)), we have \(V_T (R_T \varphi) = \int_{\Lambda} \llbracket (R_T \varphi (x))^4 \rrbracket \mathrm{d} x - 3 c_T^2 | \Lambda |\). The additive constant \(- 3 c_T^2 | \Lambda |\) does not depend on \(\varphi\) and can be absorbed in the normalisation constant \(Z_T\) of \(\nu^T\), so from now on we drop it and denote

\(\displaystyle V_T (R_T \varphi) = \int_{\Lambda} \llbracket (R_T \varphi (x))^4 \rrbracket \mathrm{d} x\)

since this corresponds to the 4-th Hermite polynomial of the random variable \(R_T \varphi (x)\).

Recall that Hermite polynomials are defined to be the orthogonal polynomials of a Gaussian measure, see e.g. [38].

A basic result is to show that the density

\(\displaystyle \exp (- \lambda V_T (R_T \varphi))\)

belongs to \(L^p (\mu^{\operatorname{GFF}})\) for all \(p \geqslant 1\) uniformly in \(T \rightarrow \infty\). The strategy of Nelson [46] is based on two observations:

  1. The first is that the polynomial \(\xi^4 - r_T \xi^2\) is bounded below by a quantity proportional to \(- r_T^2\), which in our case diverges to \(- \infty\) as \(- \log^2 T\).

  2. The second is hypercontractivity, which we take to mean the following inequality for polynomials \(P\) of order \(d\) of a Gaussian vector \(X\):

    \(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [| P |^{2 p}] \leqslant (2 p - 1)^{d p} \mathbb{E} [| P |^2]^p \) (7)

    see e.g. [38] or the lecture notes of Hairer [33].

Take \(K < T\) and write

\(\displaystyle V_T (R_T \varphi) = (V_T (R_T \varphi) - V_K (R_K \varphi)) + V_K (R_K \varphi)\)

with \(V_K (R_K \varphi) \gtrsim - \log^2 K\). Therefore for all \(l > 0\)

\(\displaystyle V_T (R_T \varphi) < - l \Rightarrow\)
\(\displaystyle (V_T (R_T \varphi) - V_K (R_K \varphi)) < - l - V_K (R_K \varphi) < - l + C \log^2 K\)

and using Markov+hypercontractivity (7),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} (V_T (R_T \varphi) < - l) \leqslant \mathbb{P} (| (V_T (R_T \varphi) - V_K (R_K \varphi)) | > l - C \log^2 K)\)
\(\displaystyle \leqslant \frac{\mathbb{E} [| V_T (R_T \varphi) - V_K (R_K \varphi) |^p]}{(l - C \log^2 K)^p}\)
\(\displaystyle \lesssim \frac{\{ \mathbb{E} [| V_T (R_T \varphi) - V_K (R_K \varphi) |^2] \}^{p / 2}}{(l - C \log^2 K)^p} \lesssim p^{2 p} K^{- p / 2} (l - C \log^2 K)^{- p} .\)

Take \(K = e^{((l - 1) / C)^{1 / 2}}\) so \(l - C \log^2 K = 1\) and

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} (V_T (R_T \varphi) < - l) \lesssim p^{2 p} e^{- (p / 2) ((l - 1) / C)^{1 / 2}} \lesssim (p^2 e^{- c l^{1 / 2}})^p,\)

and taking \(p = e^{c l^{1 / 2} / 2 - 1}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} (V_T (R_T \varphi) < - l) \lesssim e^{- 2 e^{c l^{1 / 2} / 2 - 1}}\)

from which we conclude that

\(\displaystyle \sup_T \| \exp (- \lambda V_T (R_T \varphi)) \|_{L^q (\mu)} < \infty\)

for all \(q \geqslant 1\).

3The variational approach

Gibbs measures are solutions to a variational problem: If \(\mu\) is a probability measure on a measure space \((\Omega, \mathcal{F})\) and \(V\) a bounded measurable function, bounded from below, the variational functional

\(\displaystyle F_V (\nu) := \int V \mathrm{d} \nu + H (\nu | \mu),\)

is defined for all probability measures \(\nu\) on \((\Omega, \mathcal{F})\). Here \(H\) is the relative entropy of \(\nu\) wrt. \(\mu\) defined as

\(\displaystyle H (\nu | \mu) := \sup_f \left[ \int f \mathrm{d} \nu - \log \int \exp (f) \mathrm{d} \mu \right],\)

where the supremum runs over bounded measurable functions \(f : \Omega \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\). The relative entropy is finite only if \(\nu \ll \mu\) and in this case it holds

\(\displaystyle H (\nu | \mu) = \int \log \left[ \frac{\mathrm{d} \nu}{\mathrm{d} \mu} \right] \mathrm{d} \nu .\)

From the definition one also deduces that \(H\) is non-negative and convex and that \(H (\nu | \mu) = 0\) iff \(\nu = \mu\). It is also lower semicontinuous for the weak topology as the supremum in the definition can be taken over continuous functions (see e.g. [23]).

The functional \(F_V\) is convex and it admits a unique minimum \(\nu^V\) given by

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} \nu^V = \frac{e^{- V}}{Z^V} \mathrm{d} \mu \) (8)

and

\(\displaystyle F_V (\nu^V) = - \log Z^V = - \log \int e^{- V} \mathrm{d} \mu .\)

As a consequence the Gibbs measure (8) can be characterised as the minimum of the functional \(F_V\), i.e. as the result of an optimisation problem.

When the measure \(\mu\) is a Wiener measure, the variational formula acquires a more specific form which is amenable to interesting stochastic analysis.

By a theorem of Föllmer if \((\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mu)\) is a Brownian probability space with Wiener measure \(\mu\) we have that every \(\nu\) with finite entropy \(H (\nu | \mu) < + \infty\) can be represented as a Girsanov transform, namely there exists a previsible function \(u \in L^2 (\Omega \times [0, \infty) ; \mathbb{R}^n)\) such that

\(\displaystyle H (\nu | \mu) = \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{E}_{\nu} \int_0^{\infty} | u_s |^2 \mathrm{d} s < + \infty,\)

and \(\nu = \mu^u\) with \(\mu^u\) the Girsanov shift of \(\mu\) in the direction \(u\):

\(\displaystyle \frac{\mathrm{d} \mu^u}{\mathrm{d} \mu} = \exp \left( \int_0^{\infty} u_s \mathrm{d} B_s - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} | u_s |^2 \mathrm{d} s \right),\)

where we denote \(B\) the canonical process on \((\Omega, \mathcal{F})\). By this result the variational problem takes the form

\(\displaystyle \inf_{u \in \mathbb{H}_a} \int_{\Omega} V \mathrm{d} \mu^u + \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{E}_{\mu^u} \int_0^{\infty} | u_s |^2 \mathrm{d} s. \) (9)

Under \(\mu^u\) the process \(W^u := B - I (u)\) with \(I (u)_t := \int_0^t u_s \mathrm{d} s\) is a Brownian motion and \(\operatorname{Law}_{\mu^u} (W^u) =\operatorname{Law}_{\mu} (B)\). Note that the quadratic costs above are expectations under \(\nu = \mu^u\), under which \(B\) has drift \(u\), and not under \(\mu\).

A similar formula, but based on a fixed probability space and a fixed Brownian motion, was found by Boué–Dupuis [12]. We need the notion of tame functional:

Definition 13. Let \((X, \mathcal{F}, \gamma)\) be a probability space. A measurable function \(F : X \rightarrow [- \infty, \infty]\) is called tame with respect to \(\gamma\) if there exist \(p, q > 1\) with \(\frac{1}{p} + \frac{1}{q} = 1\) and

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\gamma} [\exp (- q F)] +\mathbb{E}_{\gamma} [| F |^p] < \infty .\)

Tameness has been introduced by Üstünel [54] as a convenient condition for the validity of the Boué–Dupuis variational formula (BD formula) for Brownian functionals. It has been improved by Hariya and Watanabe [34] to allow for \(p = q = 1\).

Theorem 14. [Boué–Dupuis] Let \(V\) be a tame functional for \(\mu\), i.e. such that

\(\displaystyle \int | V |^p + e^{- q V} \mathrm{d} \mu < + \infty,\)

for some \(p, q > 1\) with \(1 / p + 1 / q = 1\), then

\(\displaystyle - \log \int e^{- V} \mathrm{d} \mu = \inf_{u \in \mathbb{H}_a} \int \left[ V (B + I (u)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| u_s \|^2 \mathrm{d} s \right] \mathrm{d} \mu .\)

Proof. This is essentially Theorem 7 from Üstünel [54]. Note that the functional \(V (B + I (u))\) makes sense for any \(u \in \mathbb{H}_a\) since by Theorem 2 from Üstünel [54] we have that the push-forward measure \(\nu = (B_{\cdot} + I (u)_{\cdot})_{\#} \mu\) has finite relative entropy with respect to \(\mu\) and therefore is absolutely continuous, i.e. \(\nu \ll \mu\).\(\Box\)

Remark 15. Some observations on these variational formulas.

  1. They originate directly from the variational formula for the free energy of statistical mechanical systems: \(V\) playing the role of the internal energy and the quadratic term playing the role of the entropy.

  2. The infimum might not be attained in Theorem 14 (see e.g. Theorem 8 in [54]) while it is attained in (9).

  3. The drift generated by absolutely continuous perturbations of the Wiener measure has been introduced and studied by Föllmer [22].

  4. They are a non–Markovian and infinite dimensional extension of the well known stochastic control problem representation of the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation in finite dimensions [21].

  5. The BD formula is easier to use than the formula (9) since the probability measure does not depend on the drift \(u\). Going from one formulation to the other requires proving that certain SDEs with functional drift admit strong solutions and that one is able to approximate unbounded functionals \(V\) by bounded ones.

  6. See Üstünel [54] and Lehec [42] for a streamlined proof of the BD formula and for applications to functional inequalities on Gaussian measures.

3.1An example

As an example application of the BD formula, consider the problem of proving the exponential integrability of a functional \(F (B)\) of a Brownian motion \((B_t)_{t \geqslant 0}\). We will assume that this functional allows the following bound

\(\displaystyle F (B + I (u)) \leqslant H (B) + L \| u \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R})}, \) (10)

for some integrable \(H (B)\) and a constant \(L > 0\). Essentially, the functional should be of linear growth along the directions of the Cameron-Martin space of the Brownian motion. In this case by BD we have, for all \(\alpha \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \log \mathbb{E}e^{\alpha F (B)} = \sup_{u \in \mathbb{H}_a} \mathbb{E} \left[ \alpha F (B + I (u)) - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| u_s \|^2 \mathrm{d} s \right]\)

where we denoted by \(\mathbb{E}\) the expectation wrt. the Wiener measure \(\mu\). Using the bound (10) for \(F\) we note that, path-wise (i.e. for almost every \(\omega \in \Omega\))

\(\displaystyle \alpha F (B + I (u)) - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| u_s \|^2 \mathrm{d} s \leqslant \alpha H (B) + \alpha L \| u \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R})} - \frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R})}^2\)
\(\displaystyle \leqslant \alpha H (B) + \sup_{\eta \geqslant 0} \left[ \alpha L \eta - \frac{1}{2} \eta^2 \right] \leqslant \alpha H (B) + \frac{L^2 \alpha^2}{2}\)

which gives a bound uniform in the control process \(u\). As a consequence we readily derive the bound

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}e^{\alpha F (B)} \leqslant \exp \left( \alpha \mathbb{E} [H (B)] + \frac{L^2 \alpha^2}{2} \right), \qquad \alpha > 0,\)

which in particular implies that \(F\) has a Gaussian upper tail. What is remarkable is that the BD variational formula allows an analytic line of reasoning (find a bound on the variational functional for all the controls) to derive a probabilistic consequence. Moreover the integrability question has been rephrased in a control of the Cameron–Martin shifts of the functional.

3.2Euclidean fields as minimisers

Consider the Gibbs measure

\(\displaystyle \theta^V (\mathrm{d} \phi) := \frac{e^{- V (\phi)} \theta^0 (\mathrm{d} \phi)}{\int e^{- V (\phi)} \theta^0 (\mathrm{d} \phi)}, \qquad \phi \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2),\) (11)

where \(\theta^0\) is the law of the Gaussian field \(\phi\) (GFF) on \(\mathbb{R}^2\) with covariance \((1 - \Delta)^{- 1}\) and where \(\Delta\) denotes the Laplacian on \(\mathbb{R}^2\). The function \(V : \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2) \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) represents an interaction potential and for now we will only assume that it is measurable and tame with respect to \(\theta^0\).

To introduce a useful variational description of this class of measures we need to enlarge our probability space and realise the GFF \(\theta^0\) as the terminal value of a Brownian martingale \((W_t)_{t \in [0, 1]}\) with values in \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2)\) and covariance given by

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [W_t (x) W_s (y)] = (t \wedge s) (1 - \Delta)^{- 1} (x - y), \qquad x, y \in \mathbb{R}^2, \quad t, s \in [0, 1] .\)

We construct this Brownian martingale on the measure \(\mathbb{P}\) given by the law of a cylindrical Brownian motion \((X_t)_{t \in [0, 1]}\) on \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\). It can be realised on \(C ([0, 1], C^{- 1 - \varepsilon} (\rho^{\varepsilon}))\) where \(\varepsilon > 0\) can be taken arbitrarily small. Let

\(\displaystyle W_t := (1 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 2} X_t, \qquad t \in [0, 1],\)

and note that indeed \(\theta^0 =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (W_1)\). We will consider \(W\) as a random variable in \(\mathfrak{S}_0 := C ([0, 1], C^{- \varepsilon} (\rho^{\varepsilon}))\).

The measure \(\theta^V\) can be then interpreted as the law of \(W_1\) under the Gibbs measure

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{Q}^V (\mathrm{d} W) = \frac{1}{Z} e^{- V (W_1)} \mathbb{P} (\mathrm{d} W) .\)

It is well known that the measure \(\mathbb{Q}^V\) is the minimiser of a Gibbs variational problem and that

\(\displaystyle - \log Z^V = - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- V (W_1)}] = \inf_{\mathbb{R}} \{ \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{R}} [V (W_1)] + H (\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{P}) \},\)

where \(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\) denotes the expectation with respect to a probability measure \(\mathbb{R}\) on the path space \(C ([0, 1], \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2))\) and \(H (\mathbb{R}|\mathbb{P})\) the relative entropy of \(\mathbb{R}\) with respect to \(\mathbb{P}\).

Let \(\mathfrak{H} (\rho^{\alpha}) = H_t^1 H_x^1 (\rho^{\alpha})\), \(\mathfrak{H} = \mathfrak{H} (\rho^0)\). Denote with \(\mathfrak{H}^a (\rho^{\alpha})\) the space of all processes \(Z : \mathfrak{S}_0 \times [0, 1] \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2)\) progressively measurable with respect to the filtration generated by \((W_t)_{t \in [0, 1]}\), starting from \(0\) and equipped with the norm of \(L^2 (\mathbb{P}, \mathfrak{H} (\rho^{\alpha}))\). Moreover define the quadratic form \(\mathcal{E}: \mathfrak{H}^a \rightarrow L^0 (\mathbb{P})\) giving the “energy” of the drift \(Z\) as

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{E} (Z) := \frac{1}{2} \int_0^1 \| (1 - \Delta)^{1 / 2} \dot{Z}_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s = \frac{1}{2} \int_0^1 (\| \nabla \dot{Z}_s \|^2_{L^2} +\| \dot{Z}_s \|^2_{L^2}) \mathrm{d} s\)

where \(\dot{Z}\) denotes the time-derivative of \(Z\).

With these definitions, the Boué–Dupuis formula [12, 54] reads as follows.

Lemma 16. (BD formula) For any tame functional \(G : \mathfrak{S}_0 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) we have

\(\displaystyle - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G (W_{\cdot})}] = \inf_{Z \in \mathfrak{H}^a} \mathbb{E} [G (W_{\cdot} + Z_{\cdot}) +\mathcal{E} (Z)] .\)

Proof. Theorem 14 specialised in our context gives

\(\displaystyle - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G (W_{\cdot})}] = \inf_{u \in \mathbb{H}^a} \mathbb{E} \left[ G (W_{\cdot} + Z_{\cdot}) + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^1 \| u_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s \right],\)

where \(\mathbb{H}^a\) is the space of all progressively measurable processes starting from \(0\) equipped with the norm of \(L^2 (\mathbb{P}, L_t^2 L^2_x)\) and

\(\displaystyle Z_t := (1 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 2} \int_0^t u_s \mathrm{d} s.\)

Observing that \(u_t = (1 - \Delta)^{1 / 2} \dot{Z}_t\) we obtain the claim.\(\Box\)

We call a random variable with values in \(\mathfrak{H}^a\) a control (or drift) and we will understand that \(W = W_1\) and \(Z = Z_1\) whenever this does not lead to confusion.

To simplify certain technical considerations later it is convenient to augment the process \(W\) with some additional information. Consider a Polish space \(\mathfrak{S}\) endowed with a continuous projection \(\pi_W : \mathfrak{S} \rightarrow \mathfrak{S}_0\) and let \(\mathbb{W}: \mathfrak{S}_0 \rightarrow \mathfrak{S}\) be a measurable map such that \(\pi_W \mathbb{W}= W\), i.e. \(\mathfrak{S} \xrightarrow{W} \mathfrak{S}_0\) is a fibered topological space and \(\mathbb{W}\) a measurable section. We will understand \(\mathbb{W}\) as an enhanced GFF. The kind of enhancements needed will be different in different models. We will always consider the space \(\mathfrak{S}\) endowed with the measure given by the law of \(\mathbb{W}\) under \(\mathbb{P}\). A functional on \(\mathfrak{S}\) is tame if it is tame for this measure.

For any measurable \(f : \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2) \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) bounded below and in \(L^2 (\theta^0)\) (not necessarily linear) define \(\mathcal{W}^V (f)\) as

\(\displaystyle e^{-\mathcal{W}^V (f)} := \int e^{- f (\phi)} \theta^V (\mathrm{d} \phi) .\)

As a direct consequence of Lemma 16, the functional \(\mathcal{W}^V\) has the variational representation

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (f) = \inf_{Z \in \mathfrak{H}^a} F^{f + V} (Z) - \inf_{Z \in \mathfrak{H}^a} F^V (Z) \)

for any \(f\) such that \(f + V\) is tame and where for a tame functional \(G\) on \(\mathfrak{S}_0\) we let

\(\displaystyle F^G (Z) := \mathbb{E} [G (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)], \qquad Z \in \mathfrak{H}^a .\)

In the formulation of Gibbs variational problem, the optimiser corresponds to the Gibbs measure. Correspondingly we would like to show that the optimiser in the BD formula, i.e. an optimal drift \(Z\) describes the law of the “interacting field” via the equality

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{Q}^V} (W) =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (W + Z) \) (12)

valid for any minimiser \(Z\) of the problem in Lemma 16 and in particular

\(\displaystyle \theta^V =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (W_1 + Z_1) .\)

The existence of such minimisers will be guaranteed, in the rigorous discussion below, by suitably relaxing the variational problem into a problem on the space of probability measures.

Before proceeding with the rigorous development, let us pause a moment to give an overview of what follows assuming that minimisers exist, then:

  1. any minimiser \(Z\) satisfies the Euler–Lagrange equation (first variation)

    \(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\nabla V (W_1 + Z_1) \cdot K_1 + 2\mathcal{E} (Z, K)] = 0 \) (13)

    for all “test controls” \(K\), where \(\nabla V (W_1 + Z_1) \cdot K_1\) denotes the derivative of \(\varphi \mapsto V (\varphi)\) at \(W_1 + Z_1\) and in the direction of \(K_1\). In the following we will refer to the equation (13) as a weak forward–backwards stochastic (partial) differential equation (wFBSDE). The reason for this name derives from observing that (13) can be formally rewritten as

    \(\displaystyle \int_0^1 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \mathbb{E} [(\nabla V (W_1 + Z_1) + (1 - \Delta) \dot{Z}_s) \dot{K}_s] \mathrm{d} s = 0\)

    and since this holds for any nice adapted control \(K\) it implies that

    \(\displaystyle (1 - \Delta) \dot{Z}_s = -\mathbb{E} [\nabla V (W_1 + Z_1) | \mathcal{F}_s] \) (14)

    which is a kind of elliptic equation for the optimal control \((Z_s)_{s \in [0, 1]}\). It can be cast into a system of a forward SDE for \(Z\) and a backward SDE which computes the conditional expectation. In these notes we will not exploit further this specific interpretation of (13) as a FBSDE so it is not necessary to make it more precise.Let us stress that our wFBSDE is a stochastic quantisation for Euclidean QFTs of a new kind.

  2. additionally, we will be able to introduce a variational description of the measure \(\mathbb{Q}^V\) with drift \(Z\) via a renormalised BD formula of the form

    \(\displaystyle - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- f (X_1)}] = \inf_K \mathbb{E} [f (X_1 + K_1) + V (X_1 + K_1) - V (X_1) +\mathcal{E} (Z + K) -\mathcal{E} (Z)] \) (15)

    with \(X_s = W_s + Z_s\) being the “interacting field” with potential \(V\) and \(f (\varphi)\) a test function which depends only locally on the field \(\varphi\) and where the infimum is taken among all drifts \(K\) which satisfy suitable decay at infinity. Given this decay one expects that the quantities \(V (X_1 + K_1) - V (X_1)\) and \(\mathcal{E} (Z + K) -\mathcal{E} (Z)\) are finite even if each summand is infinite.

This reformulation will show its full power as soon as we try to describe Euclidean quantum fields in infinite volume. In that case the original variational problem loses its meaning as the interaction potential is almost surely infinite, due to an integration over the unbounded set \(\mathbb{R}^2\).

On the other hand the first-order condition (13) remains meaningful also in this setting, as the interaction is local and can be used, very much like the weak formulation of an elliptic problem, to get estimates on the minimiser \(Z\), e.g. by testing with \(K = \rho Z\) where \(\rho : \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}_+\) is some weight function decaying at spatial infinity. In particular we will obtain estimates of the form

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \| \rho Z \|_{H^1_t H^1_x}^2 < \infty,\)

uniformly in the volume. This estimate together with the identification (12) is at the core of the proof of tightness of the family of finite volume measures \((\mathbb{Q}^{\Lambda})_{\Lambda}\) with \(\Lambda \nearrow \mathbb{R}^2\). Any accumulation point \(\mathbb{Q}\) represents an infinite volume Euclidean field, moreover the corresponding drift \(Z\) gives, via the formula (12) an explicit coupling of \(\mathbb{Q}\) and the GFF given by \(\mathbb{P}\).

The point of view which we advocate in these lectures is that the wFBSDE equation (13) provides a complete control over the drift \(Z\) describing the infinite volume Euclidean field, for which a naive variational description is meaningless. This will be exemplified by the analysis of the quartic interaction and of the exponential model in infinite volume, respectively in Section 4 and Section 5.

Moreover the renormalised cost functional \(\check{H}^V (\upsilon)\) (introduced in Lemma 27) can be made finite in the infinite volume by choosing localised perturbations \(K\). This remark will be clearer by looking at how we determine the infinite volume limit of the exponential model in Section 5.

The rest of this section is dedicated to providing a rigorous proof of these claims.

The standard approach to guarantee the existence of minimisers is to relax our problem, i.e. minimise over the law of the pair \((W, Z)\) with \(W\) fixed and \(Z\) varying over \(\mathfrak{H}^a\). In order to ensure compactness we need to complete the space of trial laws for a suitable topology for which our target functional is coercive.

Let \(\mathfrak{H}_w (\rho^{\alpha})\) be \(\mathfrak{H} (\rho^{\alpha})\) equipped with the weak topology and let \(\widehat{\mathfrak{H}} := \mathfrak{H} (\rho^{1 / 2})\), \(\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w := \mathfrak{H}_w (\rho^{1 / 2})\) and \(\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}^a := \mathfrak{H}^a (\rho^{1 / 2})\). With a slight abuse of notation we denote \((\mathbb{W}, Z)\) the canonical coordinates on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\).

Definition 17. Let

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{X} := \{ \mu =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, Z) \in \mathcal{P} (\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w) : Z \in \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}^a, \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\| Z \|_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}}^2] < \infty \},\)

and

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{X}} := \{ \mu \in \mathcal{P} (\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w) : \exists \mu_n \in \mathcal{X}, \mu_n \rightarrow \mu \text{weakly}, \sup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \mathbb{E}_{\mu_n} [\| Z \|_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}}^2] < \infty \} .\)

We equip \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) with the following notion of convergence: a sequence \((\mu_n)_n\) in \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) converges to \(\mu\) if

  1. \(\mu_n \rightarrow \mu\) weakly on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\),

  2. \(\sup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \mathbb{E}_{\mu_n} [\| Z \|_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}}^2] < \infty\).

Note that the probability space in the definition of \(\mathcal{X}\) is fixed, so all elements in \(\mathcal{X}\) are laws of random variables living on a fixed probability space. Note that all elements of \(\mathcal{X}\) have first marginal given by \(\operatorname{Law} (\mathbb{W})\), and this also carries over to \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) by weak convergence.

We will need to realise couplings of two elements of \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\). These are constructed by the next lemma which needs the following preliminary definition of the space hosting the coupling.

We denote the canonical coordinates on the product space \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\) by \((\mathbb{W}, Z, K)\).

Definition 18. For \(\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}\) let \(\overline{\mathcal{Y}} (\mu) \subseteq \mathcal{P} (\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w)\) be the set of all \(\upsilon\) such that \(\operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (\mathbb{W}, Z) = \mu\) and for which there exist sequences \((Z^n)_n, (K^n)_n \subseteq \mathfrak{\hat{H}}^a\) with

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, Z^n, K^n) \rightarrow \upsilon \text{and } \sup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \mathbb{E} [\| Z^n \|^2_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}} + \| K^n \|^2_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}}] < \infty .\)

Lemma 19. For any \(\nu, \mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}\) there exists \(\upsilon \in \overline{\mathcal{Y}} (\mu)\) such that

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (\mathbb{W}, Z + K) =\operatorname{Law}_{\nu} (\mathbb{W}, Z) .\)

Proof. Since \(\mu, \nu\) are in \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) we can find sequences \(Z^n, \bar{Z}^n \in \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}^a\) such that \(\mu_n : =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, Z^n) \rightarrow \mu\) and \(\nu_n := \operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, \bar{Z}^n) \rightarrow \nu\) weakly on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\) and furthermore \(\sup_n \mathbb{E} [\| Z^n \|_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}}^2 + \| \bar{Z}^n \|_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}}^2] < \infty\). Now define

\(\displaystyle K^n = \bar{Z}^n - Z^n,\)

and set

\(\displaystyle \upsilon^n =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, Z^n, K^n) .\)

We have that \(\sup_n \mathbb{E} [\| K^n \|^2_{\mathfrak{\hat{H}}}] \leqslant 2 \sup_n \mathbb{E} [\| Z^n \|_{\mathfrak{\hat{H}}}^2 + \| \bar{Z}^n \|_{\mathfrak{\hat{H}}}^2] < \infty\) so \(\upsilon^n\) is tight on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \mathfrak{\hat{H}}_w \times \mathfrak{\hat{H}}_w\). Taking a convergent subsequence (not relabelled) we can assume that \(\upsilon^n \rightarrow \upsilon\). We then have

\begin{eqnarray*} \operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (\mathbb{W}, Z + K) & = & \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon^n} (\mathbb{W}, Z + K)\\ & = & \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, Z^n + K^n)\\ & = & \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, \bar{Z}^n) = \nu \end{eqnarray*}

which proves the claim. \(\Box\)

On functionals on \(\bar{\mathcal{X}}\) we can define first and second order derivatives as follows. Let \(\mu \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}\) and let \(K : \mathfrak{S} \times \mathfrak{\hat{H}}_w \rightarrow \mathfrak{\hat{H}}_w\) be a measurable process adapted to the filtration generated by \((\mathbb{W}_t, Z_t)_{t \in [0, 1]}\), continuous in the second component and such that \(K \in L^2 (\mu, \mathfrak{\hat{H}})\). We call such \(K\) an admissible control for \(\mu\). For all \(\sigma \in \mathbb{R}\) let \(\mu^{\sigma K}\) be the law of \((\mathbb{W}, Z + \sigma K)\) under \(\mu\). We have \(\mu^{\sigma K} \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}\). A functional \(\check{F} : \bar{\mathcal{X}} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is differentiable in \(\bar{\mathcal{X}}\) in the direction of the admissible control \(K\) if the map \(\sigma \mapsto \check{F} (\mu^{\sigma K})\) is differentiable in \(\sigma = 0\) and in this case we define

\(\displaystyle \left. \nabla \check{F} (\mu) (K) = \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \sigma} \right|_{\sigma = 0} \check{F} (\mu^{\sigma K}) .\)

Second order derivatives can be similarly defined by taking two admissible controls \(K, K'\) and letting

\(\displaystyle \nabla^2 \check{F} (\mu) (K, K') = \left. \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \sigma'} \right|_{\sigma' = 0} \left. \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \sigma} \right|_{\sigma = 0} \check{F} (\mu^{\sigma K + \sigma' K'}),\)

whenever \((\sigma, \sigma') \mapsto \check{F} (\mu^{\sigma K + \sigma' K'})\) is \(C^2\) in \((\sigma, \sigma') = 0\).

We are ready to relax the variational problem and prove an alternative representation of \(\mathcal{W}^V (f)\).

Definition 20. We say that a functional \(\check{F} : \bar{\mathcal{X}} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is admissible if it is lower semicontinuous and such that

\(\displaystyle \check{F} (\mu) \gtrsim 1 +\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\mathcal{E} (Z)], \) (16)

this ensures in particular that the functional \(\check{F} : \bar{\mathcal{X}} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is coercive, that is for all \(L < \infty\) the level sets \(\{ \check{F} \leqslant L \}\) are compact in \(\bar{\mathcal{X}}\).

Definition 21. For a tame \(G : \mathfrak{S} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) define the functional \(\check{F}^G : \bar{\mathcal{X}} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) by

\(\displaystyle \check{F}^G (\mu) := \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G (W + Z_{}) +\mathcal{E} (Z)], \qquad \mu \in \bar{\mathcal{X}} .\)

We say that \(G\) is admissible if \(G\) is tame, \(\check{F}^G\) is admissible and \((W, Z) \rightarrow G (W + Z)\) is continuous on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\).

The following lemma is an obvious consequence of this relaxed variational setting.

Lemma 22. Assume that \(G : \mathfrak{S} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is admissible, then

\(\displaystyle \inf_{Z \in \mathfrak{H}^a} F^G (Z) = \inf_{Z \in \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}^a} F^G (Z) = \inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{X}} \check{F}^G (\mu) = \min_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^G (\mu) . \) (17)

Let \(\mu^G \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}\) be any minimiser of \(\check{F}^G\). If \(\check{F}^G\) is differentiable at \(\mu^G\) it satisfies the equation \(\nabla \check{F}^G (\mu^G) (K) = 0\) for all admissible controls \(K\). Moreover if it is twice differentiable at \(\mu^G\) we also have the positivity condition \(\nabla^2 \check{F}^G (\mu^G) (K, K) \geqslant 0\) for all admissible controls \(K\).

Proof. The equality \(\inf_{Z \in \mathfrak{H}^a} F^G (Z) = \inf_{Z \in \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}^a} F^G (Z)\) follows from the property (16) of the admissible functional \(\check{F}^G\) since \(\mathfrak{H}^a\) can be viewed as a subspace of \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) via the map \(u \mapsto \operatorname{Law} (\mathbb{W}, u)\). The equality \(\inf_{Z \in \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}^a} F^G (Z) = \inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{X}} \check{F}^G (\mu)\) follows directly from the definition of \(\mathcal{X}\). We have that \(\inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^G (\mu) = \min_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^G (\mu)\) again from the admissibility of \(G\) which ensures that sequences \((\mu_n)_n \subseteq \mathcal{X}\) of approximate minimisers of \(\check{F}^G\) converge in \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) and that any limit \(\mu^G\) is a minimiser. The first and second order conditions are then consequences of the differentiability of \(\check{F}^G\). It remains to show

\(\displaystyle \inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{X}} \check{F}^G (\mu) = \inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^G (\mu) .\)

We first show this statement for bounded \(G\). Since \(\mathcal{X} \subseteq \bar{\mathcal{X}}\) and, by Lemma 16 and the equalities proven above, \(\inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{X}} \check{F}^G (\mu) = - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G (W)}]\), it is enough to show that

\(\displaystyle \check{F}^G (\mu) \geqslant - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G (W)}], \qquad \mu \in \bar{\mathcal{X}} .\)

Fix \(\mu \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}\) with \(\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\mathcal{E} (Z)] < \infty\) (otherwise there is nothing to prove) and let \((\mathcal{F}_t)_{t \in [0, 1]}\) be the filtration generated by \((\mathbb{W}_s, Z_s)_{s \leqslant t}\) on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\). We claim that under \(\mu\) the process \(\mathbb{W}\) is still an \((\mathcal{F}_t)\)-Brownian martingale, i.e. that \(\mathbb{W}_t -\mathbb{W}_s\) is independent of \(\mathcal{F}_s\) for \(s \leqslant t\). Indeed, let \(\mu_n =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W}, Z^n) \in \mathcal{X}\) with \(\mu_n \rightarrow \mu\), let \(\psi\) be bounded and continuous and let \(\Phi\) be a bounded continuous function of finitely many of the variables \(\mathbb{W}_r, \langle Z_r, g \rangle\) with \(r \leqslant s\) and \(g\) smooth with compact support (these are continuous on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\) and generate \(\mathcal{F}_s\)). Since \(Z^n\) is adapted to the filtration of \(\mathbb{W}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\mu_n} [\psi (\mathbb{W}_t -\mathbb{W}_s) \Phi] =\mathbb{E} [\psi (\mathbb{W}_t -\mathbb{W}_s)] \mathbb{E}_{\mu_n} [\Phi],\)

and this identity passes to the limit \(n \rightarrow \infty\) by weak convergence, proving the claim. As a consequence, under \(\mu\) the process \(X := (1 - \Delta)^{1 / 2} \mathbb{W}\) is an \((\mathcal{F}_t)\)-cylindrical Brownian motion and, since \(Z\) is \((\mathcal{F}_t)\)-adapted and absolutely continuous in time, \(\dot{Z}\) admits an \((\mathcal{F}_t)\)-progressive version, so that

\(\displaystyle (1 - \Delta)^{1 / 2} (\mathbb{W}+ Z)_t = X_t + \int_0^t u_s \mathrm{d} s, \qquad u_s := (1 - \Delta)^{1 / 2} \dot{Z}_s, \qquad \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{E}_{\mu} \int_0^1 \| u_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s =\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\mathcal{E} (Z)] .\)

The entropy bound for Brownian motions with adapted drift (see e.g. [42]), which holds for drifts progressively measurable with respect to any filtration for which the Brownian motion is a Brownian motion, gives then

\(\displaystyle H (\operatorname{Law}_{\mu} (\mathbb{W}+ Z) |\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W})) \leqslant \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\mathcal{E} (Z)] .\)

By the Gibbs variational principle (for bounded \(G\)) we conclude

\(\displaystyle - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G (W)}] \leqslant \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G (W + Z)] + H (\operatorname{Law}_{\mu} (\mathbb{W}+ Z) |\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (\mathbb{W})) \leqslant \check{F}^G (\mu),\)

which is the claim.

We now remove the restriction that \(G\) is bounded. To this end let \(G^N = (G \wedge N) \vee (- N)\). Then we have already established that

\(\displaystyle - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G^N (W)}] = \inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{X}} \check{F}^{G^N} (\mu) = \min_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^{G^N} (\mu) .\)

Now let \(\nu\) be a minimiser of \(\check{F}^G\) in \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\) (existence of this was established above). Then

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G (W + Z) + \mathcal{E} (Z)] & = & \mathbb{E}_{\nu} [G (W + Z) + \mathcal{E} (Z)]\\ & = & \lim_{N \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_{\nu} [G^N (W + Z) + \mathcal{E} (Z)]\\ & \geqslant & \lim_{N \rightarrow \infty} \inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G^N (W + Z) + \mathcal{E} (Z)]\\ & = & \lim_{N \rightarrow \infty} - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G^N (W)}]\\ & = & - \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- G (W)}]\\ & = & \inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{X}} \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G (W + Z) + \mathcal{E} (Z)] \end{array}\)

where we have used dominated convergence.\(\Box\)

Remark 23. Note that

\(\displaystyle \check{F}^G (\mu^{\sigma K}) - \check{F}^G (\mu) =\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G (W + Z + \sigma K_{}) - G (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z + \sigma K) -\mathcal{E} (Z)]\)
\(\displaystyle =\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [G (W + Z + \sigma K_{}) - G (W + Z) + 2 \sigma \mathcal{E} (Z, K) + \sigma^2 \mathcal{E} (K)] .\)

If \(G\) is differentiable in the direction of \(H^1\) we have

\(\displaystyle \nabla \check{F}^G (\mu) (K) =\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\nabla G (W + Z_{}) \cdot K + 2\mathcal{E} (Z, K)] .\)

If \(G\) is twice differentiable we have also

\(\displaystyle \nabla^2 \check{F}^G (\mu) (K, K') =\mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\nabla^2 G (W + Z_{}) \cdot (K, K') + 2\mathcal{E} (K, K')] .\)

The consequences of the existence of this minimiser are clarified by the following lemmas.

Lemma 24. If \(V\) and \(f + V\) are admissible and \(\mu^{f + V}\), \(\mu^V\) are minimisers of \(\check{F}^{f + V}\) and \(\check{F}^V\) respectively, then we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\mu^{f + V}} [f (W + Z)] \leqslant \mathcal{W}^V (f) \leqslant \mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [f (W + Z)] . \) (18)

Proof. Since \(V\) and \(f + V\) are admissible we have proven that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (f) = \inf_{\mu \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^{f + V} (\mu) - \inf_{\mu \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^V (\mu) = \check{F}^{f + V} (\mu^{f + V}) - \check{F}^V (\mu^V) . \) (19)

Eq. (19) implies in turn

\(\displaystyle \check{F}^{f + V} (\mu^{f + V}) - \check{F}^V (\mu^{f + V}) \leqslant \mathcal{W}^V (f) \leqslant \check{F}^{f + V} (\mu^V) - \check{F}^V (\mu^V)\)

and since

\(\displaystyle \check{F}^{f + V} (\mu^{g + V}) - \check{F}^V (\mu^{g + V}) =\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{g + V}} [f (W + Z) + V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)]\)
\(\displaystyle -\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{g + V}} [V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)] =\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{g + V}} [f (W + Z)]\)

for any \(g\) (in particular admissible \(g\)), we obtain eq. (18).\(\Box\)

Lemma 25. Let \(V : \mathfrak{S} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) be admissible. Then for all \(f : \mathfrak{S} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) continuous and bounded \(f + V\) is admissible. Let \(\mu^{f + V}\) be a minimiser for \(\check{F}^{f + V}\), then for all \(\alpha \in \mathbb{R}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \alpha} \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) =\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\alpha f + V}} [f (W + Z)] . \) (20)

Proof. The admissibility of \(f + V\) is easy to obtain given that \(f\) is bounded and continuous and \(V\) is admissible. It is clear that \(\alpha \mapsto \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f)\) is differentiable for bounded \(f\), we need only to establish the representation of this derivative via the variational minimiser \(\mu^{\alpha f + V}\). For any \(\gamma \geqslant 0\) we have, by direct computation

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V ((\alpha + \gamma) f) -\mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) \leqslant \check{F}^{(\alpha + \gamma) f + V} (\mu^{\alpha f + V}) - \check{F}^{\alpha f + V} (\mu^{\alpha f + V}) = \gamma \mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\alpha f + V}} [f (W + Z)],\)

and

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) -\mathcal{W}^V ((\alpha - \gamma) f) \geqslant \check{F}^{\alpha f + V} (\mu^{\alpha f + V}) - \check{F}^{(\alpha - \gamma) f + V} (\mu^{\alpha f + V}) = \gamma \mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\alpha f + V}} [f (W + Z)] .\)

By differentiability of \(\mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f)\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\alpha f + V}} [f (W + Z)] \geqslant \lim_{\gamma \rightarrow 0 +} \frac{\mathcal{W}^V ((\alpha + \gamma) f) -\mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f)}{\gamma} = \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \alpha} \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f)\)

and

\(\displaystyle \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \alpha} \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) = \lim_{\gamma \rightarrow 0 +} \frac{\mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) -\mathcal{W}^V ((\alpha - \gamma) f)}{\gamma} \geqslant \mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\alpha f + V}} [f (W + Z)]\)

from which we can conclude.\(\Box\)

There are several interesting consequences of this formula. First of all, integrating (20) in \(\alpha\), we have

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (f) = \int_0^1 \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \alpha} \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) \mathrm{d} \alpha = \int_0^1 \mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\alpha f + V}} [f (W + Z)] \mathrm{d} \alpha, \) (21)

which is a representation of \(\mathcal{W}^V (f)\) alternative to the variational formula. On the other hand, taking \(\alpha = 0\) in (20) we obtain

\(\displaystyle \int f (\phi) \theta^V (\mathrm{d} \phi) = \left. \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \alpha} \right|_{\alpha = 0} \mathcal{W}^V (\alpha f) =\mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [f (W_1 + Z_1)] .\)

Theorem 26. The law of \(W_1 + Z_1\) under \(\mu^V\) is indeed \(\theta^V\). In particular for all \(f : \mathfrak{S} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) continuous and bounded

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (f) = - \log \int e^{- f (\phi)} \theta^V (\mathrm{d} \phi) = - \log \mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [e^{- f (W_1 + Z_1)}] .\)

This last formula naturally calls for a variational description of the average \(\mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [e^{- f (W_1 + Z_1)}]\) with respect to the base measure \(\mu^V\). This extension of the BD formula to the interacting field \(W + Z\) is the subject of the next lemma.

Let \(\mathcal{E} (Z, K)\) be the symmetric bilinear form associated to \(\mathcal{E} (Z)\), in such a way that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{E} (Z + K) =\mathcal{E} (Z) +\mathcal{E} (K) + 2\mathcal{E} (Z, K) .\)

Lemma 27. Let \(V\) be admissible and denote by \(\mu^V \in \bar{\mathcal{X}}\) a minimiser of \(\check{F}^V\). Then we have the following direct variational description of \(\mu^V\):

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (f) = - \log \mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [e^{- f (W + Z)}] = \inf_{\upsilon \in \bar{\mathcal{Y}} (\mu^V)} \check{G}^{f, V} (\upsilon)\)

with

\(\displaystyle \check{G}^{f, V} (\upsilon) := \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [f (W + Z + K)] + \check{H}^V (\upsilon),\)

and

\(\displaystyle \check{H}^V (\upsilon) := \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [V (W + Z + K) - V (W + Z) + 2\mathcal{E} (Z, K) +\mathcal{E} (K)] .\)

Moreover \(\nu\) is a minimiser of \(\check{F}^V\) iff \(\check{H}^V (\upsilon) \geqslant 0\) for all \(\upsilon \in \bar{\mathcal{Y}} (\nu)\) and if \(V\) is differentiable in the directions of \(H^1\) we also have the weak forward–backward SDE (wFBSDE):

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [\nabla V (W + Z) \cdot K + 2\mathcal{E} (Z, K)] = 0, \qquad \upsilon \in \bar{\mathcal{Y}} (\nu), \) (22)

and \(\check{H}^V (\upsilon)\) takes the alternative form

\(\displaystyle \check{H}^V (\upsilon) =\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [V (W + Z + K) - V (W + Z) - \nabla V (W + Z) \cdot K +\mathcal{E} (K)] .\)

Proof. Recall that

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathcal{W}^V (f) & = & \inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^{f + V} - \inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \check{F}^V\\ & = & \inf_{\mu \in \overline{\mathcal{X}}} \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [f (W + Z) + V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)] -\mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)]\\ & = & \inf_{\upsilon \in \bar{\mathcal{Y}} (\mu^V)} \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [f (W + Z + K) + V (W + Z + K) +\mathcal{E} (Z + K)]\\ & & -\mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)] \end{eqnarray*}

where in the last line we used Lemma 19 to justify the change of variables in the minimisation problem. Note that for any \(\upsilon \in \overline{\mathcal{Y}} (\mu^V)\) we have that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\mu^V} [V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)] =\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [V (W + Z) +\mathcal{E} (Z)]\)

so

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}^V (f) = \inf_{\upsilon \in \overline{\mathcal{Y}} (\mu^V)} \{ \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [f (W + Z + K)] + \check{H}^V (\upsilon) \} .\)

The fact that \(\check{H}^V (\upsilon) \geqslant 0\) iff \(\operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (W, Z) = \nu\) is a minimiser of \(\check{F}^V\) follows from its definition, indeed note that

\(\displaystyle \check{H}^V (\upsilon) = \check{F}^V (\operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (W, Z + K)) - \check{F}^V (\operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (W, Z)) .\)

If \(V\) is differentiable in the direction of \(H^1\) we have that for any minimiser \(\nu\) of \(\check{F}^V\) and any \(\upsilon \in \bar{\mathcal{Y}} (\nu)\):

\(\displaystyle 0 = \nabla \check{F}^V (\nu) (K) =\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [\nabla V (W + Z) \cdot K + 2\mathcal{E} (Z, K)]\)

which also allows us to simplify the expression of \(\check{H}^V\).\(\Box\)

4The quartic interaction

In this section we will consider the general setting of Section 3.2 in the case where the interaction potential \(V\) is given by

\(\displaystyle V_{\Lambda} (\phi) := \lambda \int_{\Lambda} \llbracket \phi^4 \rrbracket,\)

where \(\Lambda \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2\) is a measurable bounded set (of sufficient regularity, see below) and \(\llbracket \phi^n \rrbracket\) denotes the \(n\)-th Wick power of the Gaussian free field \(\phi\) [24] and \(\lambda > 0\) an arbitrary fixed constant. Let \(\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda} := \mathcal{W}^{V_{\Lambda}}\) and \(\theta_{\Lambda} := \theta^{V_{\Lambda}}\).

The measure \(\theta_{\Lambda}\) on \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^2)\) is called the \(\varphi^4_2\) Euclidean quantum field with a finite volume cutoff and we will discuss in detail the problem of the removal of the finite volume cutoff, i.e. the limit \(\Lambda \nearrow \mathbb{R}^2\). In particular:

  1. using the wFBSDE (13) we will derive a-priori bounds for minimisers strong enough to give tightness of the family \((\theta_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda}\) as \(\Lambda \nearrow \mathbb{R}^2\);

  2. we will describe any accumulation point via a formula for its Laplace transform involving the wFBSDE obtained in the previous section.

Unfortunately, establishing rigorously the form (15) of the limiting variational problem requires certain locality properties of the solutions to the wFBSDE (13) in infinite volume. This is usually possible when \(V\) is convex.
