We say that \((\upsilon^n)_n \subseteq \mathcal{Y}^M\) converges to \(\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M\) if \(\upsilon^n \rightarrow \upsilon\) weakly in \(\mathcal{P} (\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w \times \mathfrak{H}_w (\rho^{- N}))\) and

\(\displaystyle \sup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon^n} [\| Z \|^2_{\widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w} + \| K \|^2_{\mathfrak{H} (\rho^{- N})}] \leqslant 2 M.\)

Remark 54. Note that any sequence in \(\mathcal{Y}^M\) has a convergent subsequence.

Corollary 55. There exists a sufficiently large \(M = M (f)\) (not depending on \(\xi\)) such that

\(\displaystyle \inf_{\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y} (\mu^{0, \xi})} \check{G}^{f, \xi, \exp} (\upsilon) = \inf_{\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu^{0, \xi})} \check{G}^{f, \xi, \exp} (\upsilon) .\)

Proof. This is a direct consequence of the decay of the minimiser established in Lemma 52.\(\Box\)

Definition 56. Define

\(\displaystyle \bar{G}^{f, \xi, \exp} (\upsilon) = \check{G}^{f, \xi, \exp} (\upsilon) \text{if $\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^{M (f)} (\mu^{0, \xi})$} \text{and } + \infty \text{otherwise.}\)

The next lemma discusses \(\Gamma\)-convergence of the variational description as the infrared cutoff is removed.

Lemma 57. Let \((\xi_n)_n\) be a sequence of spatial cutoffs such that \(\xi_n (x) = 1\) for \(| x | \leqslant n\), and

\(\displaystyle \sup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \| \xi_n \|_{W^{k, \infty}} < + \infty, \qquad \text{for any } k \in \mathbb{N}.\)

Pick a subsequence \(\xi_n\) (not relabelled) such that \(\mu^n \rightarrow \mu\) weakly on \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\) where \(\mu^n\) is the minimiser of \(\check{F}^{0, \xi_n}\) in \(\overline{\mathcal{X}}\). Then \(\bar{G}^{f, \xi_n, \exp}\) \(\Gamma\)-converges to \(\bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp}\) on \(\mathcal{Y}^M\) where

\(\displaystyle \bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon) =\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} \left[ f (W + Z + K) + \mathcal{E} (K) + \lambda \int \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} \right], \qquad \Psi (t) := e^t - 1 - t \geqslant 0,\)

if \(\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu)\) and \(+ \infty\) otherwise. Note that \(\bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp}\) is the formal limit \(\xi \rightarrow 1\) of the reduced form of \(\check{G}^{f, \xi, \exp}\) given in Lemma 51.

Proof. Recall that, since \(\mu^n\) minimises \(\check{F}^{0, \xi_n}\), by Lemma 51 we have, for \(\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu^n)\),

\(\displaystyle \bar{G}^{f, \xi_n, \exp} (\upsilon) =\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} \left[ f (W + Z + K) + \mathcal{E} (K) + \lambda \int \xi_n \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} \right] .\)

The function \(\Psi\) is convex and non-negative, with \(\Psi (0) = 0\), decreasing on \((- \infty, 0]\) and increasing on \([0, \infty)\); in particular all the integrands above are non-negative. Fix \(p \in (1, \min (2, 8 \pi / \beta^2))\) and \(\delta\) with \(\beta^2 (p - 1) / (4 \pi) < \delta < 2 / p'\) (this interval is non-empty since \(p < 8 \pi / \beta^2\)), so that \(M^{\beta} \in B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho^{1 / 2})\) almost surely by Proposition 41, and take these values of \(p\) and \(s = \delta\) in the definition of the space \(\mathfrak{M}\) in \(\mathfrak{S} = \mathfrak{S}_0 \times \mathfrak{M}\).

Step 1 (a convergence lemma). Let \((Z^n, K^n, M^{\beta, n})\) and \((Z, K, M^{\beta})\) be random variables on a probability space \((\tilde{\Omega}, \tilde{\mathcal{F}}, \tilde{\mathbb{P}})\) such that, almost surely, \(Z^n \rightarrow Z\) and \(K^n \rightarrow K\) weakly in \(H^1_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) and \(M^{\beta, n} \rightarrow M^{\beta}\) in \(B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho^{1 / 2})\), and such that the law of \(M^{\beta, n}\) is that of \(M^{\beta}\) for all \(n\). Let \(R > 0\) and let \(\Phi : \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) be bounded, smooth in \(x\), Lipschitz in \((z, k)\) uniformly in \(x\), and such that \(\Phi (z, k, x) = 0\) for \(| x | \geqslant R\). Then

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \Phi (Z^n, K^n, x) M^{\beta, n} (\mathrm{d} x) \rightarrow \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \Phi (Z, K, x) M^{\beta} (\mathrm{d} x) . \) (35)

Indeed, let \(g^n := \Phi (Z^n, K^n, \cdot)\) and \(g := \Phi (Z, K, \cdot)\), all supported in \(B (0, R)\). Almost surely the sequences \((Z^n)_n, (K^n)_n\) are bounded in \(H^1 (B (0, R))\) (being weakly convergent), so by the chain rule \((g^n)_n\) is bounded in \(H^1\), and by Rellich's theorem and the Lipschitz property of \(\Phi\) we have \(g^n \rightarrow g\) in \(L^2\). By interpolation \(g^n \rightarrow g\) in \(H^s\) for all \(s < 1\) and, since \(p' \geqslant 2\), the Sobolev embedding \(H^s \subseteq B_{p', p'}^{s - 1 + 2 / p'}\) for functions supported in \(B (0, R)\) gives \(g^n \rightarrow g\) in \(B_{p', p'}^{\delta}\), choosing \(s \in (1 + \delta - 2 / p', 1)\). By duality, since \(\rho\) is bounded above and below on \(B (0, R)\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \int g^n \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} - \int g \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} \right| \lesssim_R \| g^n - g \|_{B_{p', p'}^{\delta}} \| M^{\beta, n} \|_{B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho^{1 / 2})} + \left| \int g \mathrm{d} (M^{\beta, n} - M^{\beta}) \right| \rightarrow 0\)

almost surely. Finally \(\left| \int g^n \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} \right| \leqslant \| \Phi \|_{L^{\infty}} \int \eta \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n}\) for a smooth bump function \(\eta \geqslant \mathbb{1}_{B (0, R)}\), and \(\int \eta \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n}\) has a law independent of \(n\) and a finite \(p\)-th moment (Proposition 41). Hence the sequence is uniformly integrable and (35) follows from Vitali's theorem.

We will use Step 1 with the following truncation. Let \(\chi \in C^{\infty}_c (\mathbb{R}^2)\) be radial, non-increasing in \(| x |\), with \(\chi = 1\) on \(B (0, 1)\) and \(\chi = 0\) outside \(B (0, 2)\), and let

\(\displaystyle \varsigma_m (z, x) := \chi (x / m) \max (- m, \min (z, (2 m - z)_+)), \qquad z \in \mathbb{R}, x \in \mathbb{R}^2 .\)

Then \(\varsigma_m\) is smooth in \(x\), 1-Lipschitz in \(z\), \(| \varsigma_m | \leqslant m\), \(\varsigma_m (z, x) = 0\) for \(| x | \geqslant 2 m\), \(\varsigma_m (z, x)\) has the sign of \(z\), and \(| \varsigma_m (z, x) | \uparrow | z |\) as \(m \rightarrow \infty\). We write \(\varsigma_m (h, x)\) for the function \(x \mapsto \varsigma_m (h (x), x)\).

Step 2 (liminf inequality). Assume that \(\upsilon^n \rightarrow \upsilon\) in \(\mathcal{Y}^M\). If \(\liminf_n \bar{G}^{f, \xi_n, \exp} (\upsilon^n) = + \infty\) there is nothing to prove; otherwise, passing to a subsequence realising the liminf, we may assume that \(\bar{G}^{f, \xi_n, \exp} (\upsilon^n) < \infty\), i.e. \(\upsilon^n \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu^n)\), for all \(n\). Then \(\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu)\): indeed \(\operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (W, M^{\beta}, Z) = \lim_n \operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon^n} (W, M^{\beta}, Z) = \lim_n \mu^n = \mu\), and property b) in the definition of \(\mathcal{Y}^M\) passes to the limit by a diagonal argument, since the approximating sequences of all the \(\upsilon^n\) satisfy the same bound \(2 M\) (on such bounded sets the weak topologies involved are metrisable). In particular \(\bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon)\) is given by the formula in the statement. From the definition of convergence in \(\mathcal{Y}^M\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon^n} [f (W + Z + K)] \rightarrow \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [f (W + Z + K)], \qquad \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [\mathcal{E} (K)] \leqslant \liminf_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon^n} [\mathcal{E} (K)],\)

the latter by the Portmanteau theorem. By the Skorokhod representation theorem [37] there exist a probability space \(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\) and random variables \((W^n, M^{\beta, n}, Z^n, K^n)\) with law \(\upsilon^n\) converging almost surely in \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w \times \mathfrak{H}_w (\rho^{- N})\) to \((W, M^{\beta}, Z, K)\) with law \(\upsilon\). By the choice of the topology of \(\mathfrak{S}\), \(M^{\beta, n} \rightarrow M^{\beta}\) in \(B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho^{1 / 2})\) almost surely, the law of \(M^{\beta, n}\) is that of \(M^{\beta}\), and \(Z^n \rightarrow Z\), \(K^n \rightarrow K\) weakly in \(H^1_{\operatorname{loc}}\) almost surely. Moreover \(Z^n \leqslant 0\) almost surely (since \(Z \leqslant 0\) \(\mu^n\)-almost surely), hence also \(Z \leqslant 0\).

For fixed \(m\) apply Step 1 with \(R = 2 m\) and \(\Phi_m (z, k, x) := e^{\beta (z \wedge 0)} \varsigma_m (\Psi (\beta k), x)\): it is bounded by \(m\), it vanishes for \(| x | \geqslant 2 m\), and it is Lipschitz in \((z, k)\) since \(\varsigma_m (\cdot, x)\) is 1-Lipschitz and vanishes when \(\Psi (\beta k) \geqslant 2 m\), so that only \(k\) in a compact interval matters, where \(\Psi (\beta \cdot)\) is Lipschitz. Since \(\xi_n = 1\) on \(B (0, 2 m)\) for \(n \geqslant 2 m\), we obtain

\(\displaystyle \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \xi_n \exp (\beta Z^n) \varsigma_m (\Psi (\beta K^n), x) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} =\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \exp (\beta Z) \varsigma_m (\Psi (\beta K), x) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} .\)

Since \(\Psi \geqslant 0\), we have \(0 \leqslant \varsigma_m (\Psi, x) \leqslant \Psi\) and \(\varsigma_m (\Psi, x) \uparrow \Psi\) as \(m \rightarrow \infty\); by monotone convergence

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} & = & \sup_m \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \exp (\beta Z) \varsigma_m (\Psi (\beta K), x) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta}\\ & = & \sup_m \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \xi_n \exp (\beta Z^n) \varsigma_m (\Psi (\beta K^n), x) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n}\\ & \leqslant & \liminf_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \xi_n \exp (\beta Z^n) \Psi (\beta K^n) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} . \end{eqnarray*}

Adding up the three terms gives \(\bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon) \leqslant \liminf_n \bar{G}^{f, \xi_n, \exp} (\upsilon^n)\). Note that, the integrand being non-negative, no separate treatment of its negative part is needed.

Step 3 (recovery sequence). Let \(\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu)\) with \(\bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon) < \infty\), so that \(\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} \int \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} < \infty\) and \(\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} \mathcal{E} (K) \leqslant M\). Set \(K^m := \varsigma_m (K, x)\) and \(\upsilon^m := \operatorname{Law}_{\upsilon} (W, M^{\beta}, Z, K^m)\). Since \(\varsigma_m\) preserves the sign and \(| \varsigma_m (K, x) | \uparrow | K |\), and \(\Psi\) is monotone on each half-line, we have \(\Psi (\beta K^m) \uparrow \Psi (\beta K)\), so by monotone convergence

\(\displaystyle \lim_{m \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} \int \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K^m) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} =\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} \int \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta},\)

while \(\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} f (W + Z + K^m) \rightarrow \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} f (W + Z + K)\) by boundedness and continuity of \(f\). We use moreover that the truncation can be performed so that \(\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [\mathcal{E} (K^m)] \rightarrow \mathbb{E}_{\upsilon} [\mathcal{E} (K)]\); we do not detail this approximation step. Then \(\bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon^m) \rightarrow \bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon)\) and, by a diagonal argument, it is enough to construct a recovery sequence for each \(\upsilon^m\).

Fix \(m\). Invoking [37] there exist a probability space \((\tilde{\Omega}, \tilde{\mathcal{F}}, \tilde{\mathbb{P}})\) and random variables \((W^n, M^{\beta, n}, Z^n)\) on it with \(\operatorname{Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} (W^n, M^{\beta, n}, Z^n) = \mu^n\) such that \((W^n, M^{\beta, n}, Z^n) \rightarrow (W, M^{\beta}, Z)\) almost surely in \(\mathfrak{S} \times \widehat{\mathfrak{H}}_w\). Let \(K\) be a random variable on \(\tilde{\Omega}\) (possibly after enlarging it) such that \(\upsilon =\operatorname{Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} (W, M^{\beta}, Z, K)\), set \(K^m = \varsigma_m (K, x)\) and \(\upsilon^{n, m} := \operatorname{Law}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} (W^n, M^{\beta, n}, Z^n, K^m)\). We claim that

\(\displaystyle \bar{G}^{f, \xi_n, \exp} (\upsilon^{n, m}) \rightarrow \bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon^m) .\)

The energy term \(\mathbb{E} [\mathcal{E} (K^m)]\) does not depend on \(n\), and \(\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} f (W^n + Z^n + K^m) \rightarrow \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} f (W + Z + K^m)\) by boundedness and continuity of \(f\). For the interaction term apply Step 1 with \(R = 2 m\), the constant sequence \(K^n = K\) and \(\Phi (z, k, x) := e^{\beta (z \wedge 0)} \Psi (\beta \varsigma_m (k, x))\), which is bounded (since \(| \varsigma_m | \leqslant m\)), Lipschitz in \((z, k)\), smooth in \(x\) and vanishes for \(| x | \geqslant 2 m\): using again \(\xi_n = 1\) on \(B (0, 2 m)\) for \(n \geqslant 2 m\) we get

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \xi_n \exp (\beta Z^n) \Psi (\beta K^m) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} \rightarrow \mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}} \int \exp (\beta Z) \Psi (\beta K^m) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} .\)

This proves the claim. Taking a diagonal sequence we obtain the statement.\(\Box\)

Corollary 58. If \((\xi_n)_n\) is as in Lemma 57 we have

\(\displaystyle \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} - \log \int \exp (- f (\varphi)) \mathrm{d} \nu^{\xi_n, \exp} = \inf_{\upsilon \in \mathcal{Y}^M (\mu)} \bar{G}^{f, \infty, \exp} (\upsilon)\)

Proof. This follows from \(\Gamma\)-convergence, the fact that \(\mathcal{Y}^M\) is sequentially compact and the fundamental theorem of \(\Gamma\)-convergence (see e.g. [18]).\(\Box\)

We conclude with some technical results needed in the proofs above.

Lemma 59. Assume that \(g : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is continuously differentiable, \(g, g'\) are bounded. Define \(f = g_-\) (the negative part of \(g\)). We have that the map

\(\displaystyle \varphi \mapsto f (\varphi),\)

continuously sends \(W_w^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})\) into itself with

\(\displaystyle \| f (\varphi) \|_{W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})} \lesssim 1 + \| \varphi \|_{W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})} .\)

If \(g (0) = 0\) we have in particular

\(\displaystyle \| f (\varphi) \|_{W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})} \lesssim \| \varphi \|_{W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})} .\)

Proof. Assume that \(g (0) = 0\). By definition of \(f\) we have

\(\displaystyle \int | f (\varphi (x)) |^2 \rho^{\alpha} (x) \mathrm{d} x \lesssim \int | \varphi (x) |^2 \rho^{\alpha} (x) \mathrm{d} x.\)

Furthermore by [19] p. 291–292, we have

\(\displaystyle \int | \nabla_x f (\varphi (x)) |^2 \rho^{\alpha} (x) \mathrm{d} x = \int \mathbb{1}_{g (\varphi (x)) \leqslant 0} | g' (\varphi (x)) \nabla_x \varphi (x) |^2 \rho^{\alpha} (x) \mathrm{d} x \lesssim \int | \nabla_x \varphi (x) |^2 \rho^{\alpha} (x) \mathrm{d} x.\)

Now for weak continuity, let \(\varphi_n \rightarrow \varphi\) weakly in \(W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})\). We know that

\(\displaystyle \sup_n \| \varphi_n \|_{W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})} < \infty \text{ which implies } \sup_n \| f (\varphi_n) \|_{W^{1, 2} (\rho^{\alpha})} < \infty .\)

Now let \(\varphi_{n_k}\) be a subsequence such that \(f (\varphi_{n_k}) \rightarrow \xi\). We can extract a further subsequence (not relabelled) such that

\(\displaystyle \varphi_{n_k} \rightarrow \varphi\)

almost surely. Then \(f (\varphi_{n_k}) \rightarrow f (\varphi)\) almost surely which in turn implies \(\xi = f (\varphi)\). From this we can conclude. The first statement follows from the second by writing \(g = \tilde{g} + g (0)\) with \(\tilde{g} (0) = 0\). \(\Box\)

Lemma 60. Let \(M^{\beta, n}\) be a sequence of random positive Radon measures on \(\mathbb{R}^2\) on a probability space \((\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})\), such that \(M^{\beta, n} \rightarrow M^{\beta}\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely in \(B_{p, p}^{- s} (\rho)\) for some \(s > \frac{\beta^2}{4 \pi} (p - 1)\) and \(p < 8 \pi / \beta^2\) close enough to \(\frac{\beta^2}{4 \pi} (p - 1), 8 \pi / \beta^2\) respectively. There exist \(q < 2\) and \(N_0 < \infty\) such that we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \int f_n \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} \rightarrow \mathbb{E} \int f \mathrm{d} M^{\beta}\)

whenever

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \left[ \| f_n - f \|^q_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})} \right] \rightarrow 0\)

and

\(\displaystyle \sup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \| f_n \|_{L^{\infty}} + \| f \|_{L^{\infty}} = C < \infty .\)

Proof. By interpolation between \(L^{\infty}\) and \(W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})\) we have that for \(\delta < \theta := q / p'\) and \(N_0 \geqslant \theta^{- 1}\) (using also the uniform \(L^{\infty}\) bound on \(f_n - f\))

\(\displaystyle \| f_n - f \|_{B_{p', p'}^{\delta} (\rho^{- 1})} \leqslant C \| f_n - f \|^{\theta}_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})}, \qquad \| f \|_{B_{p', p'}^{\delta} (\rho^{- 1})} \leqslant C \| f \|^{\theta}_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})} .\)

Now recall that for any \(\frac{8 \pi}{\beta^2} > p > 1\) and \(\delta > \frac{\beta^2}{4 \pi} (p - 1)\) we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_n \mathbb{E} [\| M^{\beta, n} \|^p_{B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho)}] < \infty,\)

so provided \(1 / p + 1 / p' = 1\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathbb{E} \left[ \int f_n \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} - \int f \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} \right] & = & \mathbb{E} \left[ \int (f_n - f) \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} \right] +\mathbb{E} \left[ \int f \mathrm{d} M^{\beta, n} - \int f \mathrm{d} M^{\beta} \right]\\ & \leqslant & \mathbb{E} [\| f_n - f \|_{B_{p', p'}^{\delta} (\rho^{- 1})} \| M^{\beta, n} \|_{B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho)}]\\ & & +\mathbb{E} [\| f \|_{B_{p', p'}^{\delta} (\rho^{- 1})} \| M^{\beta, n} - M^{\beta} \|_{B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho)}]\\ & \leqslant & \mathbb{E} \left[ C \| f_n - f \|^q_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})} \right]^{\frac{1}{p'}} \mathbb{E} [\| M^{\beta, n} \|^p_{B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho)}]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\ & & +\mathbb{E} \left[ C \| f \|^q_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})} \right]^{\frac{1}{p'}} \mathbb{E} [\| M^{\beta, n} - M^{\beta} \|^p_{B_{p, p}^{- \delta} (\rho)}]^{\frac{1}{p}} .\\ & \rightarrow & 0 \end{array}\)

since \(p' \theta = q\). Note that for any \(\beta^2 < 8 \pi\) we can choose \(q < 2\) such that

\(\displaystyle q / p' = \frac{q}{p} (p - 1) > \frac{\beta^2}{4 \pi} (p - 1)\)

so if we choose \(\delta\) such that

\(\displaystyle \frac{q}{p} (p - 1) > \delta > \frac{\beta^2}{4 \pi} (p - 1)\)

we obtain as required

\(\displaystyle \frac{8 \pi}{\beta^2} > p > 1 \qquad \delta > \frac{\beta^2}{4 \pi} (p - 1) \qquad \delta < q / p' = \frac{q}{p} (p - 1) .\)
\(\Box\)

Lemma 61. For any \(q < 2\) and any \(N_0 < \infty\) there exists an \(N < \infty\) such that if

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{L^{\infty}}, \| g \|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant C,\)

then, with a constant depending on \(C\),

\(\displaystyle \| f g \|_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})} \lesssim \| g \|_{H^1 (\rho^{- N})} + \| f \|_{H^1 (\rho)} \| g \|^{(2 - q) / q}_{H^1 (\rho^{- N})} .\)

Note that the \(L^{\infty}\) norms are unweighted. If furthermore \(f_n \rightarrow f\) in \(H^1 (\rho)\) and \(g_n \rightarrow g\) in \(H^1 (\rho^{- N})\) and \(\sup_n \| f_n \|_{L^{\infty}} + \| g_n \|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant C\) then

\(\displaystyle f_n g_n \rightarrow fg \qquad \text{in $W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})$} .\)

Proof. It is enough to prove the statement for smooth functions by density. By Hölder's inequality we have

\(\displaystyle \| f g \|_{L^q (\rho^{- N_0})} \leqslant \| f \|_{L^{\infty}} \| \rho^{N - N_0} \|_{L^r} \| g \|_{L^2 (\rho^{- N})},\)

where \(1 / r = 1 / q - 1 / 2\), so that \(r \in (2, \infty)\) and \(2 / r = (2 - q) / q\), and we take \(N \geqslant (N_0 + 1) r / 2\) (so that \(\rho^{N - N_0} \in L^r\) and \(\rho^{(N - N_0) r / 2} \lesssim \rho\), recall \(\rho \lesssim \langle \cdot \rangle^{- 32}\)). By the Leibniz rule and the same argument

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla (f g) \|_{L^q (\rho^{- N_0})} \leqslant \| f \|_{L^{\infty}} \| \rho^{N - N_0} \|_{L^r} \| \nabla g \|_{L^2 (\rho^{- N})} + \| \nabla f \|_{L^2 (\rho)} \| g \|_{L^r (\rho^{- N_0 - 1})} .\)

By interpolation between \(L^{\infty}\) and \(L^2\) (i.e. \(| g |^r \leqslant \| g \|^{r - 2}_{L^{\infty}} | g |^2\)) and since \(\rho^{- (N_0 + 1) r} \lesssim \rho^{- 2 N}\), we also have

\(\displaystyle \| g \|_{L^r (\rho^{- N_0 - 1})} \leqslant \| g \|^{1 - 2 / r}_{L^{\infty}} \| g \|^{2 / r}_{L^2 (\rho^{- (N_0 + 1) r / 2})} \lesssim C^{1 - 2 / r} \| g \|^{(2 - q) / q}_{L^2 (\rho^{- N})} .\)

This gives the estimate. For the second statement we rewrite

\(\displaystyle fg - f_n g_n = g (f - f_n) + f_n (g - g_n) .\)

The second term goes to zero by the estimate applied to \((f_n, g - g_n)\), since \(\sup_n \| f_n \|_{H^1 (\rho)} < \infty\). For the first one, proceeding as above (with the roles of the factors exchanged in the terms without derivative on \(f - f_n\)),

\(\displaystyle \| g (f - f_n) \|_{W^{1, q} (\rho^{- N_0})} \lesssim \| (f - f_n) \rho^{N - N_0} \|_{L^r} \| g \|_{H^1 (\rho^{- N})} + \| f - f_n \|_{H^1 (\rho)} \| g \|_{L^r (\rho^{- N_0 - 1})},\)

and by interpolation again \(\| (f - f_n) \rho^{N - N_0} \|_{L^r} \lesssim C^{1 - 2 / r} \| f - f_n \|^{2 / r}_{L^2 (\rho)} \rightarrow 0\).\(\Box\)

6A scale-by-scale interpolation

In this intermezzo we prepare for the analysis of the UV limit of the quartic interaction in three dimensions, i.e. for the \(\varphi^4_3\) model in finite volume \(\Lambda =\mathbb{T}^3\).

Recall that the \(\varphi^4_d\) Gibbs measure on the \(d\)-dimensional torus \(\Lambda = \Lambda_L =\mathbb{T}_L^d = (\mathbb{R}/ (2 \pi L\mathbb{Z}))^d\) is the probability measure \(\nu\) obtained as the weak limit for \(T \rightarrow \infty\) of the family \((\nu_T)_{T > 0}\) given by

\(\displaystyle \nu_T (\mathrm{d} \phi) = \frac{\exp [- V_T (\phi)]}{\mathscr{Z}_T} \vartheta_T (\mathrm{d} \phi) ,\) (36)

where

\(\displaystyle V_T (\varphi) := \lambda \int_{\Lambda} (| \varphi (\xi) |^4 - a_T | \varphi (\xi) |^2 + b_T) \mathrm{d} \xi, \qquad \mathscr{Z}_T := \int e^{- V_T (\phi_T)} \vartheta (\mathrm{d} \phi) .\)

Here \(\lambda \geqslant 0\) is a fixed constant, \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(\Lambda\), \(\vartheta\) is the centred Gaussian measure with covariance \((1 - \Delta)^{- 1}\), \(\mathscr{Z}_T\) is a normalisation factor, \(a_T, b_T\) given constants and \(\phi_T = \rho_T (\mathrm{D}) \phi\) with \(\rho_T\) some appropriate smooth and compactly supported cutoff function such that \(\rho_T \rightarrow 1\) as \(T \rightarrow \infty\), and where \(\vartheta_T := (\rho_T (\mathrm{D}))_{\ast} \vartheta\) is the law of the regularised field \(\phi_T\) under \(\vartheta\). In other words \(\nu_T\) is the law of \(\phi_T\) under the probability measure \(e^{- V_T (\phi_T)} \vartheta (\mathrm{d} \phi) / \mathscr{Z}_T\): this is the measure which is naturally controlled by the variational method below. The measures \(\vartheta\) and \(\nu_T\) are realised as probability measures on \(\mathscr{S}' (\Lambda)\), the space of tempered distributions on \(\Lambda\). They are supported on the Hölder–Besov space \(\mathscr{C}^{(2 - d) / 2 - \kappa} (\Lambda)\) for all small \(\kappa > 0\). The existence of the limit \(\nu\) is conditioned on the choice of a suitable sequence of renormalisation constants \((a_T, b_T)_{T > 0}\). The constant \(b_T\) is not necessary, but is useful to decouple the behaviour of the numerator from that of the denominator in eq. (36).

The aim of the rest of these lecture notes is to give a proof of convergence using a variational formula for the partition function \(\mathscr{Z}_T\) and for the generating function of the measure \(\nu_T\). As a byproduct we obtain also a variational description for the generating function of the limiting measure \(\nu\) via \(\Gamma\)-convergence of the variational problem.

Define the normalised free energy \(\mathcal{W}_T\) for the cutoff \(\varphi^4_d\) measure, as the functional

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}_T (f) := - \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \log \int_{\mathscr{S}' (\Lambda)} \exp [- | \Lambda | f (\phi_T) - V_T (\phi_T)] \vartheta (\mathrm{d} \phi) ,\) (37)

for all \(f \in C (\mathscr{S}' (\Lambda) ; \mathbb{R})\).

In order to use the stochastic control problem we need to embed our problem into a setting where we have a Brownian motion. How to do this is quite arbitrary and mostly shaped by technical convenience. In two dimensions, where we have very mild singularities, we have adopted a simplistic approach, introducing a Brownian martingale with GFF marginals. To tackle the three dimensional case, where singularities are more severe, it is convenient to choose an embedding where the Brownian motion encodes a scale decomposition of the underlying GFF.

We begin by constructing a probability space \((\Omega, \mathscr{B}, \mathbb{P})\) endowed with a process \((W_t)_{t \in [0, \infty]}\) belonging to \(C ([0, \infty], \mathscr{C}^{(2 - d) / 2 - \kappa} (\Lambda))\) and such that \(\operatorname{Law}_{\vartheta} (\phi_T) =\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (W_T)\) for all \(T \geqslant 0\) and \(\operatorname{Law}_{\mathbb{P}} (W_{\infty}) = \vartheta\), the Gaussian free field with covariance \((1 - \Delta)^{- 1}\).

Fix \(\alpha < - d / 2\) and let \(\Omega := C (\mathbb{R}_+ ; H^{\alpha})\), \((X_t)_{t \geqslant 0}\) the canonical process on \(\Omega\) and \(\mathscr{B}\) the Borel \(\sigma\)–algebra of \(\Omega\). On \((\Omega, \mathscr{B})\) consider the probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\) which makes the canonical process \(X\) a cylindrical Brownian motion in \(L^2 (\Lambda)\). In the following \(\mathbb{E}\) without any qualifiers will denote expectations wrt. \(\mathbb{P}\) and \(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\) will denote expectations wrt. some other measure \(\mathbb{Q}\). On the probability space \((\Omega, \mathscr{B}, \mathbb{P})\) there exists a collection \((B_t^n)_{n \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d}\) of complex (2-dimensional) Brownian motions, such that \(\overline{B^n_t} = B^{- n}_t\), \(B^n_t, B^m_t\) independent for \(m \neq \pm n\) and \(X_t = | \Lambda |^{- 1 / 2} \sum_{n \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} e^{i \langle n, \cdot \rangle} B^n_t\).

Note that \(X\) has a.s. trajectories in \(C (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathscr{C}^{- d / 2 - \varepsilon} (\Lambda))\) for any \(\varepsilon > 0\) by standard arguments.

Fix some \(\rho \in C_c^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{R}_+)\), decreasing such that \(\rho (s) = 1\) for any \(s \leqslant 1 / 2\) and \(\rho (s) = 0\) for any \(s \geqslant 1\). For \(x \in \mathbb{R}^d\), set, \(\rho_t (x) := \rho (\langle x \rangle / t)\) and

\(\displaystyle \sigma_t (x) := \left( \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} (\rho^2_t (x)) \right)^{1 / 2} = (2 \dot{\rho}_t (x) \rho_t (x))^{1 / 2} = (- 2 (\langle x \rangle / t) \rho (\langle x \rangle / t) \rho' (\langle x \rangle / t))^{1 / 2} / t^{1 / 2},\)

where \(\dot{\rho}_t\) is the partial derivative of \(\rho_t\) with respect to \(t\). Consider the process \((W_t)_{t \geqslant 0}\) defined by

\(\displaystyle W_t := \frac{1}{| \Lambda |^{1 / 2}} \sum_{n \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} \int_0^t \frac{\sigma_s (n)}{\langle n \rangle} e^{i \langle n, \cdot \rangle} \mathrm{d} B^n_s, \qquad t \geqslant 0. \) (38)

It is a centred Gaussian process with covariance

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{E} [\langle W_t, \varphi \rangle \langle W_s, \psi \rangle] & = & \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \sum_{n, m \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} \mathbb{E} \left[ \int_0^t \frac{\sigma_u (n)}{\langle n \rangle} \mathrm{d} B^n_u \hat{\varphi} (n) \overline{\int_0^s \frac{\sigma_u (m)}{\langle m \rangle} \mathrm{d} B^m_u \hat{\psi} (m)} \right]\\ & = & \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \sum_{n \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} \frac{\rho_{\min (s, t)}^2 (n)}{\langle n \rangle^2} \hat{\varphi} (n) \overline{\hat{\psi} (n)}, \end{eqnarray*}

for any \(\varphi, \psi \in \mathscr{S} (\Lambda)\) and \(t, s \geqslant 0\), by Fubini theorem and Itô isometry. By dominated convergence

\(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E} [\langle W_t, \varphi \rangle \langle W_t, \psi \rangle] = | \Lambda |^{- 1} \sum_{n \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} \langle n \rangle^{- 2} \hat{\varphi} (n) \overline{\hat{\psi} (n)}\)

for any \(\varphi , \psi \in L^2 (\Lambda)\).

Note that up to any finite time \(T\) the r.v. \(W_T\) has a bounded spectral support and the stopped process \(W^T_t = W_{t \wedge T}\) for any fixed \(T > 0\), is in \(C (\mathbb{R}_+, W^{k, 2} (\Lambda))\) for any \(k \in \mathbb{N}\). Furthermore \((W^T_t)_t\) only depends on a finite subset of the Brownian motions \((B^n)_n\). Denote

\(\displaystyle W_t = \int_0^t J_s \mathrm{d} X_s, \qquad t \geqslant 0, \) (39)

with \(J_s := \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{- 1} \sigma_s (\mathrm{D})\).

Observe that \(W_t\) has a distribution given by the push-forward \((\rho_t (\mathrm{D}))_{\ast} \vartheta\) of \(\vartheta\) through \(\rho_t (\mathrm{D})\). We write the measure \(\nu_T\) in (36) in terms of expectations over \(\mathbb{P}\) as

\(\displaystyle \int g (\phi) \nu_T (\mathrm{d} \phi) = \frac{\mathbb{E} [g (W_T) e^{- V_T (W_T)}]}{\mathscr{Z}_T}, \) (40)

for any bounded measurable \(g : \mathscr{S}' (\Lambda) \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\).

For fixed \(T\) the polynomial appearing in the expression for \(V_T (W_T)\) is bounded below (since \(\lambda > 0\)) and \(\mathscr{Z}_T\) is well defined and also bounded away from zero (this follows easily from Jensen's inequality). However as \(T \rightarrow \infty\) we tend to lose both these properties due to the fact that we will be obliged to take \(a_T \rightarrow + \infty\) to renormalise the non–linear terms. To obtain uniform upper and lower bounds we need a more detailed analysis and we proceed as follows.

Denote by \(\mathbb{H}_a\) the space of progressively measurable processes which are \(\mathbb{P}\)–almost surely in \(\mathcal{H} := L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+ \times \Lambda)\). We say that an element \(v\) of \(\mathbb{H}_a\) is a drift. Below we will need also (generalised) drifts belonging to \(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha} := L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+ ; H^{\alpha} (\Lambda))\) for some \(\alpha \in \mathbb{R}\), we denote the corresponding space with \(\mathbb{H}_a^{\alpha}\). Consider the measure \(\mathbb{Q}_T\) on \((\Omega, \mathscr{B})\) whose Radon–Nikodym derivative wrt. \(\mathbb{P}\) is given by

\(\displaystyle \frac{\mathrm{d} \mathbb{Q}_T}{\mathrm{d} \mathbb{P}} = \frac{e^{- V_T (W_T)}}{\mathscr{Z}_T} .\)

For a drift \(v \in \mathbb{H}_a^{\alpha}\) we denote by \(I (v)\) the process

\(\displaystyle I_t (v) := \int_0^t J_s v_s \mathrm{d} s, \qquad t \geqslant 0,\)

(this is the shift of \(W\) induced by the shift \(X \rightarrow X + \int_0^{\cdot} v_s \mathrm{d} s\) of the Brownian motion), and for a functional \(f\) we set \(V_T^f := V_T + | \Lambda | f\).

The Boué–Dupuis formula takes the form:

Theorem 62. For any \(f \in C (\mathscr{C}^{(2 - d) / 2 - \kappa} ; \mathbb{R})\) with linear growth the Boué–Dupuis (BD) variational formula for the free energy holds:

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}_T (f) = - \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- V_T^f (W_T)}] = \inf_{v \in \mathbb{H}_a} \mathbb{E} \left[ \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} V_T^f (W_T + I_T (v)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| v_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s \right],\)

where the expectation is taken wrt. the measure \(\mathbb{P}\) on \(\Omega\).

Remark 63. The Boué–Dupuis formula gives \(- \log \mathbb{E} [e^{- V^f_T (W_T)}] = \inf_v \mathbb{E} \left[ V_T^f (W_T + I_T (v)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \int_{\Lambda} | v_s |^2 \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} s \right]\), so that dividing by \(| \Lambda |\) produces the factor \(1 / | \Lambda |\) also in front of the quadratic cost. To keep formulas light, here and in the rest of these notes the \(L^p\), \(H^{\alpha}\) and \(\mathcal{H}\) norms appearing in the variational problems are volume-averaged, that is \(\| v \|_{L^2}^2 := _{\Lambda} | v |^2 = | \Lambda |^{- 1} \int_{\Lambda} | v |^2\), consistently with the averaged integrals \(\) used in the potentials. With this convention the formula above, and all the variational formulas below, hold as written.

The next lemma provides a deterministic regularity result for \(I (v)\): it says that the drift \(v\) generates shifts of the Gaussian free field in directions which belong to \(H^1\) uniformly in the scale parameter up to \(\infty\). The space \(H^1\) is the Cameron–Martin space of the free field [38].

Lemma 64. Let \(\alpha \in \mathbb{R}\). For any \(v \in L^2 ([0, \infty), H^{\alpha})\) we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{0 \leqslant t \leqslant T} \| I_t (v) \|_{H^{\alpha + 1}}^2 + \sup_{0 \leqslant s < t \leqslant T} \frac{\| I_t (v) - I_s (v) \|_{H^{\alpha + 1}}^2}{1 \wedge (t - s)} \lesssim \int_0^T \| v_r \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} r.\)

Proof. Using the fact that \(\sigma_s (\mathrm{D})\) is diagonal in Fourier space, and denoting with \((e_k)_{k \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d}\) the basis of trigonometric polynomials, we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \left\| \int_r^t \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) v_s \mathrm{d} s \right\|^2_{H^{\alpha}} & = & \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \sum_{k \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} \langle k \rangle^{2 \alpha} \left| \int_r^t \langle \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) e_k, v_s \rangle \mathrm{d} s \right|^2\\ & \leqslant & \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \sum_{k \in (L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z})^d} \langle k \rangle^{2 \alpha} \left( \int_r^t | \langle \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) e_k, e_k \rangle |^2 \mathrm{d} s \right) \left( \int_r^t | \langle e_k, v_s \rangle |^2 \mathrm{d} s \right)\\ & \leqslant & \int_r^t \| v_s \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s \sup_k \int_r^t \langle e_k, \sigma_s (\mathrm{D})^2 e_k \rangle \mathrm{d} s\\ & \leqslant & \int_r^t \| v_s \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s \sup_k \langle e_k, \rho_t^2 (\mathrm{D}) e_k \rangle \leqslant \int_0^T \| v_s \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s. \end{array}\)

Since \(I_t (v) - I_r (v) = \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{- 1} \int_r^t \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) v_s \mathrm{d} s\), taking \(r = 0\) this gives the bound on \(\sup_{t \leqslant T} \| I_t (v) \|_{H^{\alpha + 1}}^2\), i.e. the first part of the statement, and also the bound on the increments when \(t - r \geqslant 1\). On the other hand \(\sigma_s (\mathrm{D})\) is a smooth Fourier multiplier and using Proposition 10 we have the estimate \(\| \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) f \|_{H^{\alpha}} \lesssim \| f \|_{H^{\alpha}} / \langle s \rangle^{1 / 2}\) uniformly in \(s \geqslant 0\), therefore, for all \(0 \leqslant r \leqslant t \leqslant T\), we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \left\| \int_r^t \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) v_s \mathrm{d} s \right\|^2_{H^{\alpha}} & \leqslant & \left( \int_r^t \| \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) v_s \|_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s \right)^2 \leqslant (t - r) \int_r^t \| \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) v_s \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & (t - r) \int_0^T \| v_s \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s. \end{array}\)

We conclude that

\(\displaystyle \| I_t (v) - I_r (v) \|_{H^{\alpha + 1}}^2 \lesssim \left\| \int_r^t \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) v_s \mathrm{d} s \right\|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \leqslant [1 \wedge (t - r)] \int_0^T \| v_s \|^2_{H^{\alpha}} \mathrm{d} s.\)

\(\Box\)

6.1Two dimensions

We consider first the case \(d = 2\) assuming that the test functional \(f\) satisfies the following estimate: for sufficiently small \(\alpha > 0\)

\(\displaystyle | f (\varphi) | \leqslant C + \alpha \| \varphi \|_{B^{- \kappa}_{4, 4}}^4 . \) (41)

From Theorem 62 we see that the relevant quantity to bound is of the form

\(\displaystyle F_T (u) := \mathbb{E} \left[ f (W_T + I_T (u)) + \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} V_T (W_T + I_T (u)) + \frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 \right], \) (42)

for \(u \in \mathbb{H}_a\). Theorem 62 is stated for \(f\) with linear growth, but this is not a restriction: the truncations \(f_M := (f \wedge M) \vee (- M)\) are bounded and satisfy (41) with the same constants (since \(| f_M | \leqslant | f |\)), and by Fatou's lemma \(\mathbb{E} [e^{- | \Lambda | f (W_T) - V_T (W_T)}] \leqslant \liminf_{M \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E} [e^{- | \Lambda | f_M (W_T) - V_T (W_T)}]\), so that the bounds below, which only depend on the constants in (41), transfer from \(f_M\) to \(f\). From now on we leave implicit the integration variable over the spatial domain \(\Lambda\) and let \(Z_t = I_t (u)\) for brevity. Choosing

\(\displaystyle a_T = 6\mathbb{E} [W_T (0)^2], \qquad b_T = 3\mathbb{E} [W_T (0)^2]^2, \) (43)

we have

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} V_T (W_T + Z_T) = \lambda \llbracket W_T^4 \rrbracket + 4 \lambda \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket Z_T + 6 \lambda \llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket Z_T^2 + 4 \lambda W_T Z_T^3 + \lambda Z_T^4,\)

where

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \llbracket W_T^4 \rrbracket & := & W_T^4 - 6\mathbb{E} [W_T^2] W_T^2 + 3\mathbb{E} [W_T^2]^2,\\ \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket & := & W_T^3 - 3\mathbb{E} [W_T^2] W_T,\\ \llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket & := & W_T^2 -\mathbb{E} [W_T^2], \end{array}\)

denote the Wick powers of the Gaussian r.v. \(W_T\) [38].

These polynomials, when seen as stochastic processes in \(T\), are \(\mathbb{P}\)–martingales wrt. the filtration of \((W_t)_t\):

\(\displaystyle \llbracket W_t^n \rrbracket = n \int_0^t \llbracket W_s^{n - 1} \rrbracket \mathrm{d} W_s, \qquad n \geqslant 1.\)

In particular they have an expression as iterated stochastic integrals wrt. the Brownian motions \((B^n_t)_{t, n}\) introduced in eq. (38).

We will need to estimate the average size of the random fields \(\llbracket W_T^n \rrbracket\) uniformly in \(T\). Our computations in Section 2.3 show that for all \(\kappa > 0\) we have, for any \(N \geqslant 1\) and any \(n \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_T \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket W_T^n \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \kappa}}^N < + \infty . \) (44)

Using Theorem 62 with \(u = 0\) we readily have an upper bound for the free energy:

\(\displaystyle - \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} \log \mathscr{Z}_T \leqslant \lambda \mathbb{E} \left[ \llbracket W_T^4 \rrbracket \right] = 0.\)

For a lower bound we need to estimate from below the average under \(\mathbb{P}\) of the variational expression

\(\displaystyle \lambda \llbracket W_T^4 \rrbracket + 4 \lambda \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket Z_T + 6 \lambda \llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket Z_T^2 + 4 \lambda W_T Z_T^3 + \lambda Z_T^4 + \frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 .\)

The strategy we adopt is to bound path-wise, and for a generic drift \(u\), the contributions

\(\displaystyle \Phi_T (Z) := \underbrace{4 \lambda \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket Z_T}_{\mathrm{I}} + \underbrace{6 \lambda \llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket Z_T^2}_{\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}} + \underbrace{4 \lambda W_T Z_T^3}_{\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}},\)

in terms of quantities involving only the Wick powers of \(W\) which we can control in expectation and the last two positive terms (our “good terms”)

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 + \lambda Z_T^4 .\)

Any residual positive contribution depending on \(u\) can be dropped in the lower bound making the dependence on the drift disappear.

To control term \(\mathrm{I}\) we see that by duality and Young's inequality, for any \(\delta > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket Z_T \right| \leqslant \| \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \kappa}} \| Z_T \|_{B^{\kappa}_{1, 1}} \leqslant C_{\delta} \| \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \kappa}}^2 + \delta \| Z_T \|_{H^1}^2, \) (45)

where we used that \(\| Z_T \|_{B^{\kappa}_{1, 1}} \lesssim \| Z_T \|_{H^1}\) and Young's inequality to have a small factor \(0 < \delta \ll 1\) in front of \(\| Z_T \|_{H^1}^2\).

The other terms can be estimated with similar arguments, for example it will be useful to use interpolation and the following fractional Leibniz rule:

Proposition 65. Let \(1 < p < \infty\) and \(p_1, p_2, p_1', p_2' > 1\) such that \(\frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2} = \frac{1}{p_1'} + \frac{1}{p_2'} = \frac{1}{p}\). Then for every \(s, \alpha \geqslant 0\) there exists a constant \(C\) such that

\(\displaystyle \| \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^s (f g) \|_{L^p} \leqslant C \| \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{s + \alpha} f \|_{L^{p_2}} \| \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{- \alpha} g \|_{L^{p_1}} + C \| \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{s + \alpha} g \|_{L^{p_1'}} \| \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{- \alpha} f \|_{L^{p_2'}} .\)

The end product of these analytic bounds are estimates

\(\displaystyle | \Phi_T (Z) | \leqslant Q_T + \delta \left[ \frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 + \lambda Z_T^4 \right], \) (46)

where

\(\displaystyle Q_T = O (\lambda^2) [1 + \| \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \varepsilon}} + \| \llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket \|_{C^{- \varepsilon}} + \| W_T \|_{C^{- \varepsilon}}]^K\)

for some integer \(K \geqslant 1\). It remains to bound the term involving \(f\), which we do with (41). Since \(\| \Delta_j g \|_{L^4} \lesssim \| g \|_{L^4}\) uniformly in \(j\), we have \(\| g \|_{B^{- \kappa}_{4, 4}} \leqslant C_0 \| g \|_{L^4}\), with \(C_0\) independent of \(\Lambda\) (all norms being normalised by the volume). Hence, using \((a + b)^4 \leqslant 8 (a^4 + b^4)\),

\(\displaystyle f (W_T + Z_T) \geqslant - C - 8 \alpha \| W_T \|^4_{B^{- \kappa}_{4, 4}} - 8 \alpha C_0^4 Z_T^4 \geqslant - C - 8 \alpha \| W_T \|^4_{B^{- \kappa}_{4, 4}} - \delta \lambda Z_T^4,\)

provided \(8 \alpha C_0^4 \leqslant \delta \lambda\), which is our smallness condition on \(\alpha\). We include the term \(C + 8 \alpha \| W_T \|^4_{B^{- \kappa}_{4, 4}}\) in \(Q_T\); this does not affect the bounds below, since \(\| W_T \|_{B^{- \kappa}_{4, 4}} \lesssim \| W_T \|_{C^{- \kappa / 2}}\), whose moments are bounded uniformly in \(T\) by (44). Therefore (for \(\delta < 1 / 2\))

\(\displaystyle F_T (u) \geqslant -\mathbb{E} [Q_T] + (1 - 2 \delta) \mathbb{E} \left[ \frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 + \lambda Z_T^4 \right] \geqslant -\mathbb{E} [Q_T] .\)

This last average does not depend anymore on the drift and using (44) we end up with

\(\displaystyle \sup_T \mathbb{E} [Q_T] < \infty .\)

We have established that

\(\displaystyle \sup_T \mathbb{E} [e^{- | \Lambda | f (W_T) - V_T (W_T)}] \leqslant \sup_T \exp (| \Lambda | \mathbb{E} [Q_T]) < + \infty \) (47)

which in particular is enough to establish tightness for the family of measures \((\nu_T)_T\), as (apply (47) to \(- f / | \Lambda |\), which still satisfies (41))

\(\displaystyle \sup_T \int e^{f (\varphi)} \nu_T (\mathrm{d} \varphi) = \sup_T \frac{\mathbb{E} [e^{f (W_T) - V_T (W_T)}]}{\mathbb{E} [e^{- V_T (W_T)}]} \leqslant \sup_T \frac{\mathbb{E} [e^{f (W_T) - V_T (W_T)}]}{e^{-\mathbb{E}V_T (W_T)}} < + \infty .\)

We can also observe that the argument leading to (47) remains valid upon replacing \(V_T\) by \(p V_T\) for any \(p \geqslant 1\). This implies that \(e^{- V_T (W_T)}\) is in all the \(L^p\) spaces wrt. the measure \(\mathbb{P}\) uniformly in \(T\) and for any \(p \geqslant 1\). Together with the a.s. convergence of the martingale \((V_T (W_T))_T\) (which is bounded in all \(L^p\) spaces), this allows to conclude also the existence of the limit measure

\(\displaystyle \nu^{\infty} = \lim_T \nu_T,\)

such that

\(\displaystyle \frac{\mathrm{d} \nu^{\infty}}{\mathrm{d} \vartheta} (W_{\infty}) = \frac{e^{- V_{\infty} (W_{\infty})}}{\mathscr{Z}_{\infty}}, \qquad \mathscr{Z}_{\infty} := \mathbb{E} [e^{- V_{\infty} (W_{\infty})}] = \lim_T \mathscr{Z}_T,\)

where \(V_{\infty} (W_{\infty}) = \lim_T V_T (W_T)\) (a.s. and in all \(L^p (\mathbb{P})\)); this limit is the Wick ordered quartic potential of \(W_{\infty}\), hence a measurable function of \(W_{\infty}\), whose law is \(\vartheta\).

7The quartic interaction in three dimensions

For the quartic interaction in three dimensions the strategy we used in two dimensions fails: the Wick powers are less regular: \(\llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket \in \mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}\) uniformly in \(T\) for any small \(\kappa > 0\) and \(\llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket\) does not allow uniform bounds as a well-defined random distribution.

By this we mean that, for any \(\alpha > 3 / 2\) and \(N \geqslant 1\), the best bound we can find looks like

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \| \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket \|_{B^{- \alpha}_{\infty, \infty}}^N \lesssim (\log T)^{N / 2},\)

and this logarithmic divergence cannot be removed (for \(\alpha < 3 / 2\) the moments even diverge polynomially in \(T\)).

As a consequence there is no straightforward approach to control the terms

\(\displaystyle \llbracket W_T^3 \rrbracket Z_T, \qquad \text{and} \qquad \llbracket W_T^2 \rrbracket Z_T^2, \) (48)

like we did in two dimensions.

The only a-priori estimate on the regularity of \(Z_T = I_T (u)\) is in \(H^1\), coming from Lemma 64 and the quadratic term in the variational functional \(F_T (u)\).

An additional substantial difference is that we expect further divergences beyond the Wick ordering which have to be subtracted in order for the limiting measure to be non-trivial. For these reasons in the energy \(V_T\) we introduce further scale dependent renormalisation constants \(\gamma_T, \delta_T\) to have

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{| \Lambda |} V_T^f (Y_T) = f (Y_T) + (\lambda \llbracket Y_T^4 \rrbracket - \lambda^2 \gamma_T \llbracket Y_T^2 \rrbracket - \delta_T), \) (49)

where the Wick products \(\llbracket Y_T^4 \rrbracket, \llbracket Y^2_T \rrbracket\) are taken with respect to the variance of \(W_T\) (as opposed to \(Y_T\)).

Repeating the computation from Section 6.1 we arrive at

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} F_T (u) & = & \mathbb{E} \left[ f (W_T + Z_T) + \lambda \mathbb{W}_T^3 Z_T + \frac{\lambda}{2} \mathbb{W}^2_T Z_T^2 + 4 \lambda W_T Z_T^3 \right]\\ & & -\mathbb{E} \left[ 2 \lambda^2 \gamma_T W_T Z_T + \lambda^2 \gamma_T Z_T^2 + \delta_T \right] +\mathbb{E} \left[ \lambda Z_T^4 + \frac{1}{2} \| u \|^2_{\mathcal{H}} \right] . \end{array} \) (50)

where we introduced the convenient notations

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{W}_t^3 := 4 \llbracket W_t^3 \rrbracket, \qquad \mathbb{W}^2_t := 12 \llbracket W_t^2 \rrbracket, \qquad t \geqslant 0,\)

and we recall that \(f\) is a fixed function belonging to \(C (\mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa} ; \mathbb{R})\) with linear growth.

We will perform a change of variables in the variational functional in order to obtain some explicit cancellations which will leave only quantities well behaved as \(T \rightarrow \infty\). The main drawback is that the functional will have a less compact and canonical form.

We are going to absorb the mixed terms (48) via the quadratic cost function \(\frac{1}{2} \| u \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2\). For the first we have, via Ito formula and then completing the square

\(\displaystyle \lambda \mathbb{W}_T^3 Z_T + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| u_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s = \lambda \int_0^T \mathbb{W}_t^3 \dot{Z}_t \mathrm{d} t + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| u_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s + \text{martingale}\)
\(\displaystyle = - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T (\mathbb{W}_t^{\langle 3 \rangle})^2 \mathrm{d} t + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| w_s \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s + \text{martingale},\)

where we used that \(J_t\) is self-adjoint, set \(\mathbb{W}_t^{\langle 3 \rangle} := J_t \mathbb{W}_t^3\) and let

\(\displaystyle w_t := u_t + \lambda \mathbb{1}_{t \leqslant T} \mathbb{W}_t^{\langle 3 \rangle}, \qquad t \geqslant 0. \) (51)

Taking into account (here and below) that the martingale term will average to zero, we have replaced the divergent term \(\mathbb{W}_T^3 Z_T\) with a divergent but purely stochastic term \(\int_0^T (\mathbb{W}_t^{\langle 3 \rangle})^2 \mathrm{d} t\) which does not affect anymore the variational problem and can be explicitly removed by adding its average to \(\delta_T\).

The price to pay for this change of variables is that now the cost functional is relative to \(w\) and not \(u\) anymore. This is an important feature of the variational approach in three dimensions as we do not expect anymore that the limiting measure is absolutely continuous wrt. the GFF.

Defining

\(\displaystyle K_t := I_t (w), \qquad \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} := \int_0^T J_s \mathbb{W}_s^{\langle 3 \rangle} \mathrm{d} s\)

from (51) we deduce the relation

\(\displaystyle Z_T = - \lambda \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} + K_T,\)

which can be used to expand the second mixed divergent term in (48) as

\(\displaystyle \frac{\lambda}{2} \mathbb{W}^2_T Z_T^2 = \frac{\lambda^3}{2} \mathbb{W}^2_T (\mathbb{W}_T^{[3]})^2 - \lambda^2 \mathbb{W}^2_T \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} K_T + \frac{\lambda}{2} \mathbb{W}^2_T K_T^2 . \) (52)

The first term on the r.h.s. is a purely stochastic object and will give a contribution independent of the drift \(u\) and absorbed in \(\delta_T\).

We are still not done since this operation has left two new divergent terms on the r.h.s. of eq. (52): the \(H^1\) regularity of \(K_T\) (guaranteed by the cost functional \(\frac{1}{2} \| w \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2\)) is not enough to control the products with \(\mathbb{W}^2\) which has regularity \(\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}\), a bit below \(- 1\).

In order to proceed further we will isolate the divergent parts of these products via a paraproduct decomposition.

Let us pause for a moment in order to introduce paraproducts. The basic idea is that a product of Besov functions \(f, g\) naturally decomposes in various contributions involving products of LP blocks:

\(\displaystyle f g = \sum_{i, j} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g = \sum_{i \ll j} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g + \sum_{i \gg j} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g + \sum_{i \approx j} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g\)

where we split contributions above and below a thick diagonal of the double sum. The key observation is that if \(i \ll j\) then the product \(\Delta_i f \Delta_j g\) has Fourier transform supported in an annulus at scale \(2^j\), while if \(i \approx j\) then the same product is a-priori only spectrally supported in a ball of radius \(\approx 2^j\). These different spectral supports imply a different behaviour of the bilinear maps:

\(\displaystyle f \prec g = \sum_{i \ll j} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g\)

and

\(\displaystyle f \circ g = \sum_{i \approx j} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g.\)

Definition 66. Let \(f, g \in \mathcal{\mathscr{S}} (\Lambda)\). We define the paraproducts and resonant product

\(\displaystyle f \succ g = g \prec f := \sum_{j < i - 1} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g, \qquad \text{and} \qquad f \circ g := \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} \Delta_i f \Delta_j g.\)

Then

\(\displaystyle fg = f \prec g + f \circ g + f \succ g.\)

The basic analytic bounds for the paraproduct and resonant terms are

Proposition 67. For any polynomial weight \(\rho\), \(\beta \leqslant 0, \alpha \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(p_1, p_2 \in [1, \infty]\), \(\frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2} = \frac{1}{p}\) we have the estimate

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| f \succ g \|_{B^{\alpha + \beta}_{p, q} (\rho)} & \lesssim & \| f \|_{B^{\alpha}_{p_1, \infty} (\rho)} \| g \|_{B^{\beta}_{p_2, q} (\rho)} . \end{array}\)

For any \(\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}\) such that \(\alpha + \beta > 0\) the estimate

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| f \circ g \|_{B^{\alpha + \beta}_{p, q} (\rho)} & \lesssim & \| f \|_{B^{\alpha}_{p_1, \infty} (\rho)} \| g \|_{B^{\beta}_{p_2, q} (\rho)} . \end{array}\)

For a proof see Theorem 3.17 and Remark 3.18 in [45].

Proposition 68. For any polynomial weights \(\nu, \rho \text{ and } \beta \leqslant 0, \alpha \in \mathbb{R}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| f \succ g \|_{B^{\alpha + \beta}_{p, q} (\rho^p \nu)} & \lesssim & \| f \|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha} (\rho)} \| g \|_{B^{\beta}_{p, q} (\nu)} . \end{array}\)

The proof is an easy modification of the proof of Theorem 3.17 in [45].

Proposition 69. Assume \(m \leqslant 0\), \(\alpha \in (0, 1), \beta \in \mathbb{R}\). Let \(\eta\) be an \(S^m\) multiplier and \(q, p_1, p_2 \in [1, \infty]\), \(\frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2} = \frac{1}{p}\), \(f \in B_{p_1, \infty}^{\beta}\), \(g \in B_{p_2, \infty}^{\alpha}\). Then for any \(\delta > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \| \eta (\mathrm{D}) (f \succ g) - (\eta (\mathrm{D}) f \succ g) \|_{B_{p, q}^{\alpha + \beta - m - \delta}} \lesssim \| f \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\beta}} \| g \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\alpha}} .\)

The constant depends only on \(\alpha, \beta, \delta\) and the constants in (5).

For a proof see [3] Lemma 2.99.

To continue our algebraic analysis we expand

\begin{eqnarray*} - \lambda^2 \mathbb{W}^2_T \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} K_T + \frac{\lambda}{2} \mathbb{W}^2_T K_T^2 & = & \lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ Z_T) K_T - \lambda^2 (\mathbb{W}^2_T \circ \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]}) K_T\\ & & - \lambda^2 (\mathbb{W}^2_T \prec \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]}) K_T + \frac{\lambda}{2} (\mathbb{W}^2_T \prec K_T) K_T\\ & & + \frac{\lambda}{2} \left( (\mathbb{W}^2_T \circ K_T) K_T - (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ K_T) K_T \right) . \end{eqnarray*}

The first two terms will require renormalisations which we put in place below. All the other terms will be well behaved and we collect them in \(\Upsilon^{(1)}_T\). In particular we observe that the last one can be rewritten as

\(\displaystyle \frac{\lambda}{2} \left( (\mathbb{W}^2_T \circ K_T) K_T - (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ K_T) K_T \right) = - \frac{\lambda}{2} \mathfrak{K}_2 (\mathbb{W}^2_T, K_T, K_T)\)

introducing the trilinear form \(\mathfrak{K}_2\) whose properties are detailed in Proposition 74 below.

As we anticipated, the resonant term \(\mathbb{W}^2_T \circ \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]}\) requires renormalisation. In the expression of \(F_T\) in (50) we have the counterterm \(- 2 \lambda^2 \gamma_T W_T Z_T\) available, which we put now in use writing

\(\displaystyle - \lambda^2 (\mathbb{W}^2_T \circ \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]}) K_T - 2 \lambda^2 \gamma_T W_T Z_T = - \lambda^2 \underbrace{(\mathbb{W}^2_T \circ \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} + 2 \gamma_T W_T)}_{\mathbb{W}^{2 \diamond [3]}_T} K_T + 2 \lambda^3 \gamma_T W_T \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} .\)

The first contribution is collected in \(\Upsilon^{(6)}_T\) and the expectation of the second will contribute to \(\delta_T\).

As far as the term \(\lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ Z_T) K_T\) is concerned, we want to absorb it into \(\int \| w_s \|^2 \mathrm{d} s\). Before we can do this we must be sure that, after applying Itô's formula, it will be still possible to use \(Z^4_T\) to control some of the growth of this term. Indeed the quadratic dependence in \(K_T\) (via \(Z_T\)) cannot be fully taken care of by the quadratic cost \(\int \| w_s \|^2 \mathrm{d} s\).

We need to introduce a regularisation which makes compatible Fourier cutoffs with \(L^4\) estimates. To introduce such a regularisation fix smooth functions \(\tilde{\theta}, \eta : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb{R}_+\) such that \(\tilde{\theta} (\xi) = 1\) if \(| \xi | \leqslant 1 / 4\) and \(\tilde{\theta} (\xi) = 0\) if \(| \xi | \geqslant 1 / 3\), \(\eta (\xi) = 1\) if \(| \xi | \leqslant 1\) and \(\eta (\xi) = 0\) if \(| \xi | \geqslant 2\). Set \(\tilde{\theta}_t (\xi) := \tilde{\theta} (\xi / t)\), then define

\(\displaystyle \theta_t (\xi) = (1 - \eta (\xi)) \tilde{\theta}_t (\xi) + \zeta (t) \eta (\xi) \tilde{\theta}_t (\xi)\)

where \(\zeta (t) : \mathbb{R}_+ \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is a smooth function such that \(\zeta (t) = 0\) for \(t \leqslant 10\) and \(\zeta (t) = 1\) for \(t \geqslant 11\).

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \theta_t (\xi) \sigma_s (\xi) & = & \text{$0$ for $s \geqslant t$,}\\ \theta_t (\xi) & = & \text{$1$ for $| \xi | \leqslant c t$ for some $c > 0$ provided that $t \geqslant 11$.} \end{array} \) (53)

By the Mihlin-Hörmander theorem, the operator \(\theta_t = \theta_t (\mathrm{D})\) is bounded on \(L^p\) for any \(1 < p < \infty\).

In the following, for any \(f \in C ([0, \infty], \mathscr{S}' (\Lambda))\) we define \(f^{\flat}_t := \theta_t f_t\) then

\(\displaystyle Z_t^{\flat} = \theta_t Z_t = \int_0^t \theta_t \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{- 1} \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) u_s \mathrm{d} s = \int_0^T \theta_t \langle \mathrm{D} \rangle^{- 1} \sigma_s (\mathrm{D}) u_s \mathrm{d} s = \theta_t Z_T .\)

In this way we have \(\| Z_t^{\flat} \|_{L^p} \lesssim \| Z_T \|_{L^p}\) for all \(t \leqslant T\). In the sequel we will always assume \(T \gg 1\).

Now decompose

\(\displaystyle \lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ Z_T) K_T = \lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ Z^{\flat}_T) K_T + \lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ (Z_T - Z^{\flat}_T)) K_T\)

and use the fact that the functions \(Z_T - Z_T^{\flat}\) and \(K_T - K_T^{\flat}\) are spectrally supported outside of a ball of radius \(c T\) to show that the second term is nice enough as \(T \rightarrow \infty\) to not require further analysis and we collect it in \(\Upsilon^{(2)}_T\).

For the first we apply Itô's formula to decompose it along the flow of scales as

\(\displaystyle \lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ Z^{\flat}_T) K_T = \lambda \int_0^T (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t) \dot{K_t} \mathrm{d} t + \lambda \int_0^T (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ \dot{Z}^{\flat}_t) K_t \mathrm{d} t + \text{martingale} .\)

The second term will be fine and we collect it in \(\Upsilon^{(3)}_T\).

We are left with the singular term \(\int_0^T (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t) \dot{K_t} \mathrm{d} t\). We again absorb it by completing the square together with the cost \(\frac{1}{2} \| w \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2\):

\(\displaystyle \lambda \int_0^T (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t) \dot{K_t} \mathrm{d} t + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| w_t \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} t = - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T (J_t (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t))^2 \mathrm{d} t + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^{\infty} \| l_t \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} t\)
\(\displaystyle = - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T (J_t (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t)) (J_t (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t)) \mathrm{d} t + \frac{1}{2} \| l \|^2_{\mathcal{H}} \) (54)

where we introduced

\(\displaystyle l_t := u_t + \lambda \mathbb{1}_{t \leqslant T} \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_t + \lambda \mathbb{1}_{t \leqslant T} J_t (\mathbb{W}_t^2 \succ Z_t^{\flat}) . \) (55)

To renormalise the first term on the r.h.s. of (54) we observe that the remaining counterterm can be rewritten as

\(\displaystyle - \lambda^2 \gamma_T Z^2_T = - \lambda^2 \gamma_T (Z^{\flat}_T)^2 - 2 \lambda^2 \gamma_T Z^{\flat}_T (Z_T - Z^{\flat}_T) - \lambda^2 \gamma_T (Z_T - Z^{\flat}_T)^2 . \) (56)

Differentiating in \(T\) the first term in the r.h.s. of eq. (56) we get

\(\displaystyle - \lambda^2 \gamma_T (Z^{\flat}_T)^2 = - \lambda^2 \int_0^T \dot{\gamma}_t (Z^{\flat}_t)^2 \mathrm{d} t - 2 \lambda^2 \int_0^T \gamma_t Z^{\flat}_t \dot{Z}_t^{\flat} \mathrm{d} t. \) (57)

The last term in eq. (57) and the last two contributions in (56) are collected in \(\Upsilon^{(5)}_T\). The first contribution in eq. (57) has the right form to be used as a counterterm for the resonant product in (54).

Using the commutator \(\mathfrak{K}_{3, t}\) described in Proposition 75 below we have

\(\displaystyle - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T [(J_t (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t)) (J_t (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ Z^{\flat}_t)) + 2 \dot{\gamma}_t (Z^{\flat}_t)^2] \mathrm{d} t\)
\(\displaystyle = - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T \underbrace{[(J_t \mathbb{W}^2_t) \circ (J_t \mathbb{W}^2_t) + 2 \dot{\gamma}_t]}_{\mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle}} (Z^{\flat}_t)^2 \mathrm{d} t - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T \mathfrak{K}_{3, t} (\mathbb{W}^2_t, \mathbb{W}^2_t, Z^{\flat}_t, Z^{\flat}_t) \mathrm{d} t\)

and we collect both terms in \(\Upsilon^{(6)}_T\).

Finally, we are left with the cubic term which we rewrite as

\(\displaystyle 4 \lambda W_T Z^3_T = - 4 \lambda^4 W_T (\mathbb{W}_T^{[3]})^3 + 12 \lambda^3 W_T (\mathbb{W}_T^{[3]})^2 K_T - 12 \lambda^2 W_T \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} K^2_T + 4 \lambda W_T K^3_T .\)

The average of the first term is collected in \(\delta_T\) while all the remaining terms are collected in \(\Upsilon^{(4)}_T\). At last we have established the claimed decomposition since the residual cost functional, from eq. (54) is indeed \(\| l \|^2_{\mathcal{H}} / 2\).

Collecting all the terms, we proved the following

Lemma 70. The functional \(F_T (u)\) defined in eq. (50) takes the form

\begin{eqnarray*} F_T (u) & = & \mathbb{E} \left[ \Phi_T (\mathbb{W}, Z, K) + \lambda (Z_T)^4 + \frac{1}{2} \| l \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 \right], \end{eqnarray*}

where

\(\displaystyle w_t := u_t + \lambda \mathbb{1}_{t \leqslant T} \mathbb{W}_t^{\langle 3 \rangle}, \quad Z_t = I_t (u), \quad K_t = I_t (w),\)

\(\displaystyle l_t := u_t + \lambda \mathbb{1}_{t \leqslant T} \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_t + \lambda \mathbb{1}_{t \leqslant T} J_t (\mathbb{W}_t^2 \succ Z_t^{\flat}),\)

while the stochastic objects are collected in the vector \(\mathbb{W}\) defined as

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{W} := (\mathbb{W}^1, \mathbb{W}^2, \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}, \mathbb{W}^{[3] \circ 1}, \mathbb{W}^{2 \diamond [3]}, \mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle}),\)

with \(\mathbb{W}^1 := W\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_t := J_t \mathbb{W}_t^3, \quad \mathbb{W}_t^{[3]} := \int_0^t J_s \mathbb{W}_s^{\langle 3 \rangle} \mathrm{d} s, \quad \mathbb{W}_t^{[3] \circ 1} := \mathbb{W}^1_t \circ \mathbb{W}_t^{[3]},\)

\(\displaystyle \quad \mathbb{W}^{2 \diamond [3]}_t := \mathbb{W}^2_t \circ \mathbb{W}_t^{[3]} + 2 \gamma_t \mathbb{W}^1_t, \quad \mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle}_t := (J_t \mathbb{W}^2_t) \circ (J_t \mathbb{W}^2_t) + 2 \dot{\gamma}_t .\)

and moreover the potential has the explicit form

\(\displaystyle \Phi_T (\mathbb{W}, Z, K) := f (W_T + Z_T) + \sum_{i = 1}^6 \Upsilon^{(i)}_T,\)

with

\begin{eqnarray*} \Upsilon^{(1)}_T & := & - \frac{\lambda}{2} \mathfrak{K}_2 (\mathbb{W}^2_T, K_T, K_T) + \frac{\lambda}{2} (\mathbb{W}^2_T \prec K_T) K_T - \lambda^2 (\mathbb{W}^2_T \prec \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]}) K_T,\\ \Upsilon^{(2)}_T & := & \lambda (\mathbb{W}^2_T \succ (Z_T - Z^{\flat}_T)) K_T,\\ \Upsilon^{(3)}_T & := & \lambda \int_0^T (\mathbb{W}^2_t \succ \dot{Z}^{\flat}_t) K_t \mathrm{d} t,\\ \Upsilon^{(4)}_T & := & 4 \lambda W_T K^3_T - 12 \lambda^2 W_T \mathbb{W}_T^{[3]} K^2_T + 12 \lambda^3 W_T (\mathbb{W}_T^{[3]})^2 K_T,\\ \Upsilon^{(5)}_T & := & - 2 \lambda^2 \gamma_T Z^{\flat}_T (Z_T - Z^{\flat}_T) - \lambda^2 \gamma_T (Z_T - Z^{\flat}_T)^2 - 2 \lambda^2 \int_0^T \gamma_t Z^{\flat}_t \dot{Z}_t^{\flat} \mathrm{d} t,\\ \Upsilon^{(6)}_T & := & - \lambda^2 \mathbb{W}^{2 \diamond [3]}_T K_T - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T \mathbb{W}_t^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle} (Z^{\flat}_t)^2 \mathrm{d} t - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^T \mathfrak{K}_{3, t} (\mathbb{W}^2_t, \mathbb{W}^2_t, Z^{\flat}_t, Z^{\flat}_t) \mathrm{d} t, \end{eqnarray*}

for a suitable value of the renormalisation constant \(\delta_T\).

We do not need to include \(\mathbb{W}^{[3]}\) in the vector \(\mathbb{W}\) since it can be obtained as a function of \(\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}\) thanks to the bound

\(\displaystyle \|\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_t -\mathbb{W}^{[3]}_s \|_{\mathscr{C}^{1 / 2 - 2 \kappa}} \leqslant \int_s^t \|J_r \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_r \|_{\mathscr{C}^{1 / 2 - 2 \kappa}} \mathrm{d} r \leqslant \left[ \int_0^T \|J_r \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_r \|_{\mathscr{C}^{1 / 2 - 2 \kappa}}^2 \mathrm{d} r \right]^{1 / 2} | t - s |^{1 / 2}\)
\(\displaystyle \leqslant \left[ \int_0^T \|\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_r \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}}^2 \frac{\mathrm{d} r}{\langle r \rangle^{1 + 2 \kappa}} \right]^{1 / 2} | t - s |^{1 / 2} \lesssim \sup_{r \in [0, T]} \|\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}_r \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}} | t - s |^{1 / 2},\)

valid for all \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant t \leqslant T\) which shows that the deterministic linear map \(\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle} \mapsto \mathbb{W}^{[3]}\) is continuous from \(C ([0, \infty], \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa})\) to \(C^{1 / 2} ([0, \infty], \mathscr{C}^{1 / 2 - 2 \kappa})\).

The path-wise regularity of all the other stochastic objects follows from the next lemma, provided the function \(\gamma\) is chosen appropriately.

Lemma 71. There exists a function \(\gamma_t \in C^1 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{R})\) such that

\(\displaystyle | \gamma_t | + \langle t \rangle | \dot{\gamma}_t | \lesssim \log \langle t \rangle, \qquad t \geqslant 0, \) (58)

and such that the vector \(\mathbb{W}\) is almost surely in \(\mathfrak{S}\) where \(\mathfrak{S}\) is the Banach space

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{S}= C ([0, \infty], \mathfrak{W}) \cap \{ \mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle} \in L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}), \mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle} \in L^1 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathscr{C}^{- \kappa}) \}\)

with

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{W}=\mathfrak{W}_{\kappa} := \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa} \times \mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa} \times \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa} \times \mathscr{C}^{- \kappa} \times \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa} \times \mathscr{C}^{- \kappa},\)

and equipped with the norm

\(\displaystyle \| \mathbb{W} \|_{\mathfrak{S}} := \| \mathbb{W} \|_{C ([0, \infty], \mathfrak{W})} + \|\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle} \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa})} + \|\mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle} \|_{L^1 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathscr{C}^{- \kappa})} .\)

The norm \(\| \mathbb{W} \|_{\mathfrak{S}}\) belongs to all \(L^p (\mathbb{P})\) spaces. Moreover the averages of the Besov norms \(B^{\alpha}_{q, r}\) of the components of \(\mathbb{W}\) of regularity \(\alpha\) are uniformly bounded in the volume \(| \Lambda |\) if \(q < \infty\) (with the \(L^q\) norms normalised by the volume).

Proof. The proof is based on the observation that one can choose \(\gamma\) in such a way that every component \(\mathbb{W}^{(i)}\) of the vector \(\mathbb{W}\) is such that \((\Delta_q \mathbb{W}_t^{(i)} (x))_{t \geqslant 0}\) for \(q \geqslant - 1\) and \(x \in \Lambda\) is a martingale wrt. the Brownian filtration (possibly modulo a deterministic term we can control). This can be seen by writing these terms as iterated stochastic integrals. For example, introducing the notation \(\mathrm{d} w_s (k) = \langle k \rangle^{- 1} \sigma_s (k) \mathrm{d} B_s^k\) we can write

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{W}_T^2 (x) & = & 24 \sum_{k_1, k_2} e^{i (k_1 + k_2) \cdot x} \int_0^T \int_0^{s_2} \mathrm{d} w_{s_1} (k_1) \mathrm{d} w_{s_2} (k_2) \end{eqnarray*}

so, recalling the definition of Littlewood-Paley kernels \(\varrho_i\), we have

\(\displaystyle \Delta_i \mathbb{W}_T^2 (x) = 24 \sum_{k_1, k_2} e^{i (k_1 + k_2) \cdot x} \varrho_i (k_1 + k_2) \int_0^T \int_0^{s_2} \mathrm{d} w_{s_1} (k_1) \mathrm{d} w_{s_2} (k_2) .\)

By Burkholder's inequality and Fubini's theorem

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathbb{E} [\sup_{t \leqslant T} \| \Delta_i \mathbb{W}_t^2 \|^p_{L^p}] & \lesssim & \left( \sum_{k_1, k_2} \varrho_i (k_1 + k_2) \int_0^T \int_0^{s_2} \frac{\sigma^2_{s_1} (k_1)}{\langle k_1 \rangle^2} \frac{\sigma^2_{s_2} (k_2)}{\langle k_2 \rangle^2} \mathrm{d} s_1 \mathrm{d} s_2 \right)^{p / 2}\\ & \lesssim & 2^{p (2 + \kappa) i / 2}, \end{array}\)

uniformly in \(T\) and so

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{E} [\sup_{t \leqslant T} \| \mathbb{W}_t^2 \|^p_{B_{p, p}^{- 1 - \kappa}}] & \leqslant & \mathbb{E} \left[ \left( \sum_i 2^{p (- 1 - \kappa) i} \sup_{t \leqslant T} \| \Delta_i \mathbb{W}_t^2 \|^p_{L^p} \right) \right]\\ & \lesssim & \sum_i 2^{p (- 1 - \kappa) i} \mathbb{E} [\sup_{t \leqslant T} \| \Delta_i \mathbb{W}_t^2 \|^p_{L^p}]\\ & \lesssim & \sum_i 2^{p (- 1 - \kappa) i} 2^{p (1 + \kappa / 2) i} \lesssim \sum_i 2^{- pi \kappa / 2} < + \infty \end{eqnarray*}

By Besov embedding this implies that \(\mathbb{E} [\sup_{T < \infty} \| \mathbb{W}_T^2 \|^p_{B_{p, q}^{- 1 - \kappa}}]\) is finite for any \(p, q < \infty\) uniformly in the volume and \(\mathbb{E} [\| \mathbb{W}_T^2 \|_{C \mathscr{C}^{- 1 -}}^p]\) is finite. Since \(\mathbb{W}_T^2\) is a continuous, \(L^2\)-bounded martingale, it converges and therefore it belongs to \(C ([0, \infty], \mathscr{C}^{- 1 -})\). The same reasoning can be carried out for the more complicated terms \(\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}, \mathbb{W}^{[3] \circ 1}, \mathbb{W}^{2 \diamond [3]}, \mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle}\). The details can be found in Section 7.5.\(\Box\)

For convenience of the reader we summarise the probabilistic estimates in Table 1.

\(\mathbb{W}^1\) \(\mathbb{W}^2\) \(\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle}\) \(\mathbb{W}^{[3]}\) \(\mathbb{W}^{[3] \circ 1}\) \(\mathbb{W}^{2 \diamond [3]}\) \(\mathbb{W}^{\langle 2 \rangle \diamond \langle 2 \rangle}\)
\(C \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 -}\) \(C \mathscr{C}^{- 1 -}\) \(C \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 -} \cap L^2 \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 -}\) \(C \mathscr{C}^{1 / 2 -}\) \(C \mathscr{C}^{0 -}\) \(C \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 -}\) \(C \mathscr{C}^{0 -} \cap L^1 \mathscr{C}^{0 -}\)

Table 1. Regularities of the various stochastic objects, the domain of the time variable is understood to be \([0, \infty]\). Estimates in these norms hold a.s. and in \(L^p (\mathbb{P})\) for all \(p \geqslant 1\) (see Lemma 71).

Remark 72. The requirement that \(\mathbb{W}^{\langle 3 \rangle} \in L^2 \mathscr{C}^{- 1 / 2 -}\) will be used in Section 7.3 to establish equicoercivity and to relax the variational problem to a suitable space of measures.

7.1Commutators

We collect here the analysis of the various commutators introduced above. The properties of these commutators are crucial to the analysis of the resonant products in the paracontrolled analysis above. As such they have an important role in the argument. Their proof, however, is a routine application of basic ideas of harmonic analysis and LP decompositions. We decided to report the proofs as it can be useful for the reader to study them in order to adapt the ideas to other contexts.

Proposition 73. Let \(\alpha \in (0, 1)\), \(\beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}\) such that \(\beta + \gamma < 0\), \(\alpha + \beta + \gamma > 0\) and \(p_1, p_2, p_3, p \in [1, \infty]\) such that \(\frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2} + \frac{1}{p_3} = \frac{1}{p}\). Then there exists a trilinear form \(\mathfrak{K}_1 (f, g, h)\) such that

\(\displaystyle \| \mathfrak{K}_1 (f, g, h) \|_{B_{p, \infty}^{\alpha + \beta + \gamma}} \lesssim \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, \infty}^{\gamma}},\)

and when \(f, g, h \in \mathscr{S}\) it has the form

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{K}_1 (f, g, h) = (f \succ g) \circ h - g (f \circ h) .\)

Proof. The proof is a slight modification of the one given in [28]. Write \(S_m g := \sum_{- 1 \leqslant k < m} \Delta_k g\), so that \(f \succ g = \sum_{\ell} S_{\ell - 1} g \Delta_{\ell} f\). For smooth \(f, g, h\) we have \(f \circ h = \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} \Delta_j f \Delta_i h\) and similarly for \((f \succ g) \circ h\). Therefore, fixing an integer \(N \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{K}_1 (f, g, h) = \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} A_j \Delta_i h - \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} \sum_{k \geqslant j + N} \Delta_k g \Delta_j f \Delta_i h,\)

where \(A_j := \Delta_j (f \succ g) - (S_{j + N} g) \Delta_j f\). Since the Fourier transform of \(S_{\ell - 1} g \Delta_{\ell} f\) is supported in an annulus \(2^{\ell} \mathcal{A}\), there is \(N_0 \geqslant 1\) such that \(\Delta_j (S_{\ell - 1} g \Delta_{\ell} f) = 0\) for \(| \ell - j | > N_0\); moreover \(\Delta_j \Delta_{\ell} = 0\) for \(| \ell - j | > 1\). Hence

\(\displaystyle A_j = \sum_{| \ell - j | \leqslant N_0} ([\Delta_j, S_{\ell - 1} g] \Delta_{\ell} f + (S_{\ell - 1} g - S_{j + N} g) \Delta_j \Delta_{\ell} f) .\)

By Lemma 2.97 from [3], \(\| [\Delta_j, a] b \|_{L^r} \lesssim 2^{- j} \| \nabla a \|_{L^{p_1}} \| b \|_{L^{p_2}}\) with \(\frac{1}{r} = \frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2}\), while, since \(\alpha \in (0, 1)\), Bernstein's inequality gives \(\| \nabla S_{\ell - 1} g \|_{L^{p_1}} \lesssim 2^{\ell (1 - \alpha)} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}}\) and \(\| S_{\ell - 1} g - S_{j + N} g \|_{L^{p_1}} \lesssim 2^{- j \alpha} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}}\) for \(| \ell - j | \leqslant N_0\). Together with \(\| \Delta_{\ell} f \|_{L^{p_2}} \lesssim 2^{- \ell \beta} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}}\) this yields

\(\displaystyle \| A_j \|_{L^r} \lesssim 2^{- j (\alpha + \beta)} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}} .\)

Now we estimate the two sums separately. For fixed \(j\) all the factors of \(\sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} A_j \Delta_i h\) have frequencies of order at most \(2^j\), so this function has a Fourier transform supported in \(2^j B\) for some ball \(B\) independent of \(j\). Since \(\alpha + \beta + \gamma > 0\), by the summation lemma for functions with Fourier support in dyadic balls (Lemma 2.84 in [3]) it is enough to get an estimate on

\(\displaystyle \sup_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{(\alpha + \beta + \gamma) j} \left\| \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} A_j \Delta_i h \right\|_{L^p}\)

to bound the first sum in \(B_{p, \infty}^{\alpha + \beta + \gamma}\), so by Hölder inequality,

\begin{eqnarray*} \left\| \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} A_j \Delta_i h \right\|_{L^p} & \lesssim & \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} \| A_j \|_{L^r} \| \Delta_i h \|_{L^{p_3}} \lesssim \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} 2^{- j (\alpha + \beta)} 2^{- i \gamma} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, \infty}^{\gamma}}\\ & \lesssim & 2^{- j (\alpha + \beta + \gamma)} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, \infty}^{\gamma}} . \end{eqnarray*}

For the second term observe that for fixed \(k\) the Fourier transform of

\(\displaystyle \sum_{- 1 \leqslant j \leqslant k - N} \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} \Delta_k g \Delta_j f \Delta_i h\)

is supported in \(2^k B\). Now we can estimate again by Hölder inequality

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left\| \sum_{- 1 \leqslant j \leqslant k - N} \sum_{| i - j | \leqslant 1} \Delta_k g \Delta_j f \Delta_i h \right\|_{L^p}\\ & \lesssim & 2^{- \alpha k} \sum_{- 1 \leqslant j \leqslant k - N} 2^{- (\beta + \gamma) j} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, \infty}^{\gamma}}\\ & \lesssim & 2^{- k (\alpha + \beta + \gamma)} \| g \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| f \|_{B_{p_2, \infty}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, \infty}^{\gamma}}, \end{eqnarray*}

where in the last step we used \(\beta + \gamma < 0\). The same summation lemma (with \(k\) in place of \(j\)) then bounds the second sum in \(B_{p, \infty}^{\alpha + \beta + \gamma}\), which concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

Proposition 74. Assume \(\beta \in (0, 1)\), \(\alpha, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}\) such that \(\alpha + \gamma < 0\), and \(\alpha + \beta + \gamma = 0\), \(\frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2} + \frac{1}{p_3} = 1\) and \(\frac{1}{q_1} + \frac{1}{q_2} = 1\). Then there exists a trilinear form \(\mathfrak{K}_2 (f, g, h)\) for which

\(\displaystyle | \mathfrak{K}_2 (f, g, h) | \lesssim \| f \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| g \|_{B_{p_2, q_1}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, q_2}^{\gamma}},\)

and on smooth functions

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{K}_2 (f, g, h) = [(f \succ g) h - g (f \circ h)] .\)

Proof. This is a modification of the proof of Lemma A.6 in [25]. Repeating an algebraic computation given in the proof of that lemma, we get that for smooth \(f, g, h\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{K}_2 (f, g, h) = \left( \sum_{j \leqslant i - 1, | i - k | \leqslant L} - \sum_{i \sim k, j < i + L} \right) (\Delta_i f \Delta_j g \Delta_k h),\)

for some \(L \geqslant 1\). Then we estimate

\begin{eqnarray*} | \mathfrak{K}_2 (f, g, h) | & \lesssim & \sum_{i \sim j \sim k} \| \Delta_i f \Delta_j g \Delta_k h \|_{L^1}\\ & \lesssim & \sum_{i \sim j \sim k} \| \Delta_i f \|_{L^{p_1}} \| \Delta_j g \|_{L^{p_2}} \| \Delta_k h \|_{L^{p_3}}\\ & \lesssim & \sup_i \left( 2^{\alpha i} \| \Delta_i f \|_{L^{p_1}} \right) \sum_{j \sim k} 2^{(\beta + \gamma) k} \| \Delta_j g \|_{L^{p_2}} \| \Delta_k h \|_{L^{p_3}}\\ & \lesssim & \| f \|_{B_{p_1, \infty}^{\alpha}} \| g \|_{B_{p_2, q_1}^{\beta}} \| h \|_{B_{p_3, q_2}^{\gamma}} . \end{eqnarray*}
\(\Box\)

Proposition 75. There exists a family \((\mathfrak{K}_{3, t})_{t \geqslant 0}\) of bounded multilinear forms on \(\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa} \times \mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa} \times H^{1 / 2 - \kappa} \times H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}\) such that for smooth \(\varphi, \psi, g^{(1)}, g^{(2)}\) it holds

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{K}_{3, t} (\varphi, \psi {,} g^{(1)}, g^{(2)}) = [J_t (\varphi \succ g^{(1)}) J_t (\psi \succ g^{(2)}) - (J_t \varphi \circ J_t \psi) g^{(1)} g^{(2)}],\)

and

\(\displaystyle | \mathfrak{K}_{3, t} (\varphi, \psi, g^{(1)}, g^{(2)}) | \lesssim \frac{1}{\langle t \rangle^{1 + \delta}} \| \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \| g^{(2)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} .\)

Proof. Here \(\delta > 0\) is a small parameter which we may (and do) take with \(\delta \leqslant \kappa\), so that \(\langle t \rangle^{- 1 - \kappa} \leqslant \langle t \rangle^{- 1 - \delta}\). Note that \(\langle t \rangle^{1 / 2} J_t\) satisfies the assumptions of Proposition 69 with \(m = - 1\), therefore using also Proposition 7

\(\displaystyle \| J_t (\varphi \succ g^{(1)}) - J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - 3 \kappa}} \lesssim \langle t \rangle^{- 1 / 2} \| \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} .\)

Therefore

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| [J_t (\varphi \succ g^{(1)}) - (J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)})] J_t (\psi \succ g^{(2)}) \right|\\ & \lesssim & \| J_t (\varphi \succ g^{(1)}) - J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - 3 \kappa}} \| J_t (\psi \succ g^{(2)}) \|_{H^{- 1 / 2 + 3 \kappa}}\\ & \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- 1 / 2} \| \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \langle t \rangle^{- 1 / 2 - \delta} \| \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(2)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \end{eqnarray*}

and by symmetry also

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| [J_t (\varphi \succ g^{(1)}) J_t (\psi \succ g^{(2)}) - (J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)}) (J_t \psi \succ g^{(2)})] \right|\\ & \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- 1 - \delta} \| \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \| \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(2)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \end{eqnarray*}

Furthermore from Proposition 74 and for sufficiently small \(\kappa > 0\),

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| (J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)}) (J_t \psi \succ g^{(2)}) - ((J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)}) \circ J_t \psi) g^{(2)} \right|\\ & \lesssim & \| J_t \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 2 \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \| J_t \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- \kappa}} \| g^{(2)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}}\\ & \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- 1 - \delta} \| \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \| \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(2)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} . \end{eqnarray*}

Applying Proposition 73 we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| (J_t \varphi \succ g^{(1)}) \circ J_t \psi - (J_t \varphi \circ J_t \psi) (g^{(1)}) \|_{H^{- 1 / 2 + \kappa}}\\ & \lesssim & \| J_t \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 2 \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \| J_t \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- \kappa}}\\ & \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- 1 - \delta} \| \varphi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \| g^{(1)} \|_{H^{1 / 2 - \kappa}} \| \psi \|_{\mathscr{C}^{- 1 - \kappa}} \end{eqnarray*}

and putting things together gives the required estimate.\(\Box\)

7.2Bounds

The aim of this section is to give upper and lower bounds on \(\mathcal{W}_T (f)\) uniformly in \(T\).

The symbol \(Q_T\) will denote a generic random variable measurable wrt. \(\sigma ((W_t)_{t \in [0, T]})\) and belonging to \(L^p (\mathbb{P})\) uniformly in \(T\) and \(| \Lambda |\) for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\). This is a convenient way to express the dependence of bounds on the norms of the stochastic object \(\mathbb{W}\).

Lemma 76. For any small \(\varepsilon > 0\) there exists \(\delta > 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle | \Upsilon^{(1)}_T | + T^{\delta} | \Upsilon^{(2)}_T | + | \Upsilon^{(3)}_T | + | \Upsilon^{(4)}_T | + | \Upsilon^{(6)}_T | \leqslant C_{\varepsilon, \delta} Q_T + \varepsilon [\sup_{0 \leqslant t \leqslant T} \| K_t \|^2_{H^{1 - \delta}} + \lambda \| Z_T \|^4_{L^4}] .\)
